# PoolManager network risk report

State on 2026-11-27: cycles 1 to 29 are merged. The ledger holds 163 findings, 2,473 checks that came out safe, and 556 open leads across the 21 networks. About 500 agents have worked on it so far, roughly half of them as independent verifiers.

## Opcodes and the pool math

On 24 November the review turned to one narrower question: does any network compute an opcode, or run control flow, differently from Ethereum in a way that changes the PoolManager's arithmetic or logic? The full account is in `OPCODES.md`. No difference was found.

- 185,529 opcode test vectors gave the specified result on all 21 networks. They include 8,908 inputs built to hit a wrong `MULMOD` result that one release of the integer library under reth based nodes shipped in October 2026.
- The deployed PoolManager bytecode, placed with the same empty state on every network, answered 550 scripts of swaps, liquidity changes, hook callbacks and token transfers byte for byte as on Ethereum in 10,985 of 11,000 comparisons. The other 15 stop at a swap that ran out of gas on one side.
- 99,903 mined swaps satisfy the tick relation of `TickMath`, and 85,278 match the single-step formulas of `SwapMath` to the unit. Of 229 multi-step swaps that were replayed step by step from chain storage, 228 match to the unit and one could not be read.
- The node source of Monad, BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Avalanche, Tempo, MegaETH and Arc shows no opcode that departs from the specification. BNB Smart Chain carried a bytecode fusion feature with real faults until v1.7.8 and removed it in v1.8.0.

- This checkout builds to a PoolManager of 24,009 bytes whose creation code hash equals the one in `snapshots/PoolManagerTest.json`. A reference interpreter written for the review, with no client code in it, gives the same answer as each of the 21 networks on 1,993 scripts that execute 98.75 percent of the built bytecode and take 338 of its 387 conditional jumps both ways, apart from scripts a node could not afford in gas. Its step trace equals the traces of Tempo, Ink and the HyperEVM archive client over 110.7 million steps, and on 4,000 random pools the build's swap and liquidity amounts equal the source's formulas.

- Cycle 10 went to the code that produces blocks where a simulated call cannot reach it. Monad's compiled multiplication was transcribed instruction by instruction and matches on every tick of the range. MegaETH's sequencer runs a closed compiling executor that public RPC nodes do not re-run; 53,811 mined swaps and 7,627 receipts show no divergence. A Go compiler miscompile affects the toolchain of five networks' nodes, and none of their opcode or state code has the shape that triggers it.

- Cycle 11 checked what block production wrote. 1,493 mined liquidity removals and fee collections on Monad, MegaETH, Tempo, Arc and HyperEVM paid exactly what the source owes, to the unit. On MegaETH, HyperEVM and Polygon the receipts of about 50,000 more transactions equal a second execution, including payments made inside frames that later reverted. Monad's compiled settlement opcodes were transcribed and match, and 13,635 completed unlocks in mined blocks net to zero. The cache in front of `KECCAK256` on reth based nodes cannot return the hash of another input.

- Cycle 12 closed the Polygon question that cycle 11 had raised. bor keeps the first read of each address's code for the whole attempt at a transaction, so a parallel worker cannot store one contract's jump destination map under the PoolManager's code hash, and no jump in the PoolManager could be redirected by a wrong map in any case. The same holds for the code caches of BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche, Nitro, the reth based clients and Monad. A sixth script set took 83 conditional jumps to the side no script had taken before, and all 21 networks answer as the reference does. 3,083 more mined liquidity removals on eight networks, including removals inside transactions that also swap, paid what the source owes to the unit. The arithmetic of the builds that settle the Arbitrum family and World Chain was transcribed and matches every vector.

- Cycle 13 replayed every mined swap it collected, with no sampling: 70,053 swaps on all 21 networks, 63,848 as a single step and 6,205 through the whole swap loop from the state of the parent block. All match to the unit, and the 869 unexplained samples kept from earlier cycles replay as well. 7,837 mined transactions on Monad, MegaETH and HyperEVM were rerun in the reference interpreter and equal the block's call frames, receipts and storage, apart from the two deployments of the PoolManager itself. The Rust arithmetic that proves state for OP Mainnet, Unichain, Ink and Base was compiled for its real machine (MIPS64 and RISC-V), run in emulators, and matches every vector. The PoolManager calls out at four places only, and at each the target is chosen by the party whose own action fails if the target burns gas. Tempo moved to a new build during the cycle; the change to its interpreter was read, and every check was rerun on the new build with the same results, step traces included. The opcode checks were also rerun on the other 20 networks at the builds they report today, and nothing changed.

- Cycle 14 settled what mined data on Monad is worth. Public Monad nodes interpret, and validators may run compiled code. Every node compares its own result for each block (state root, receipts root, logs bloom, gas used) with the one the validators certified and stops if they differ, so mined results stand for the validators too. 629,293 mined swaps were checked there and 129,567 go through the 512 bit branch of `FullMath.mulDiv`; a replay of the newest 40,000 blocks leaves none unmatched. The same branch has mined instances that match on MegaETH (3,741) and HyperEVM (493). The proof program image that settles OP Mainnet, Unichain and Ink was scanned on every path and its own shift handlers run on 92,871 vectors. A reth cache fault is still in the code of seven networks; it cannot touch the storage of the PoolManager or of any contract a pool already uses.

- Cycle 15 answered whether Monad's validators run the PoolManager as compiled code. They do. The emitter was built from source, and the native code of the deployed PoolManager was run against Monad's own interpreter on about 2.2 million calls, including every path no mined transaction has used (`clear`, `donate`, the allowance paths, the overflow fallback, amounts above 2^160). Results, gas and state are equal, and planted faults were caught. The code generator of the compiler that reth ships was transcribed and checked on 631,353 vectors with no mismatch. 33,760 more mined swaps on HyperEVM match the source formulas. The 49 conditional jumps with a side no script takes were reread against the source: every one is unreachable or ends in a revert on all networks. Two findings were added. Polygon's fork of 8 December 2026 replaces the code of five accounts by a mechanism that governance once used on a pooled token (F-134, governance level). From Tempo's T15 fork a `take` to a zone deposit address goes to a zone portal, and if the deposit fails, the refund lands in the PoolManager, where an account with callbacks in the same portal batch can claim it as its own payment (F-135, low: the taker has lost that refund either way, and no pool funds are reachable).
- Cycle 16 closed two gaps of cycle 15 and tested one assumption on mined data. Monad's native PoolManager code was rebuilt on the exact dependencies of both current releases: it agrees with the interpreter on 20,450 scenarios, and 2,007 mined transactions replayed through it equal the chain in output, gas, storage and logs. MegaETH's public validator was built and reproduced the closed sequencer's state root on 43,645 blocks, among them every block with a PoolManager liquidity removal, fee collection or claim burn; a planted fault was caught. Mined transactions in which an `unlock` is undone and a later one completes were found on seven networks and rerun in the reference interpreter with equal results, and every kind of halt was constructed on all 21 networks without a surviving write. The search for the F-135 pattern on other networks gave one finding (F-137, low): a bridge delivery that anyone may execute, and that pays a recipient the sender named, can be run by any account between its own `sync` and `settle` when that recipient is the PoolManager. It applies to CCTP mints without a destination caller and to several canonical bridge relays on 17 networks. The money is the bridger's, and no pool or claim balance moves. One operator-level note was added for Elysium's Go toolchain (F-136, info). A new keccak cache in an unreleased Polygon client build was read and holds full copies of its inputs, so it cannot return another input's hash.
- Cycle 17 added no finding and closed most of what cycle 16 left open. The five sites of Monad's native PoolManager code that no scenario had run were executed in the deployed contract's own blocks on both releases; native code and interpreter agree at each, and a fault planted at each site is caught. The script sets were rerun at the real PoolManager address of all 21 networks, with the contract's code and storage left as on chain: every difference from Ethereum is the PoolManager's own state or a limit already known. The large-offset vectors ran on the 18 networks that had not had them, with no mismatch. An audit of the reference interpreter found seven faults, none on a path the PoolManager uses; the tool is corrected, and 21 whole-transaction reruns that cycle 13 had put down to gas now match. The ruint advisory does not reach HyperEVM's replay clients, and the reth cache fault has no path that feeds it on Celo, X Layer or BNB Chain's reth client, so F-132 and F-133 are rated informational now. MegaETH's validator reproduced 7,286 more blocks, among them blocks with PoolManager calls that leave no log. Three operator-level notes on Go toolchains were folded into F-117 and F-136. Seven HyperEVM swaps of another exchange did not reproduce from the source formulas and the unit kept no record of them. A rerun after the cycle saved them: each sits in a block with a mint event variant the collector did not read, and with that event applied all seven reproduce to the unit.
- Cycle 18 added one finding, and it needs chain governance. On 30 October 2026 the Arbitrum Security Council moved frozen funds with a temporary bridge contract that let it name the sender of a message. Nitro runs a contract transaction from the parent chain without checking a nonce or whether the sender has code, so the same lever could name the PoolManager and move every pooled balance without `unlock` (F-138, high, governance; it also holds for the owners of Robinhood Chain and Elysium). An ordinary account cannot do this through the stock bridge contract, which rewrites every sender. That statement was read from source in cycle 18 and confirmed against the deployed contracts in cycle 19. The rest of the cycle closed four items of the open list. Seven Go client trees were searched for the code shapes of two compiler faults and hold neither on the opcode or state path. Monad v0.16.5 writes a block's exit state in an order that depends on memory layout, and every order stores the same words, by proof from the source and by 920 forced-order builds. The 16 full-range swap scripts that endpoints had cut short now finish on Polygon, Monad and Tempo and equal Ethereum word for word. No transaction on MegaETH (72,184 traced), Monad (30,092), Unichain (28,261) or BNB Chain (8,716) ends with a currency still synced, and every `settle` returned what the source predicts. The rest of the HyperEVM swap sample was rerun: 63,540 swaps match to the unit and none does not. On Celo, all 403 mined liquidity removals were recomputed, and none differs.
- Cycle 19 added one finding that needs the upgrader of a bridge, and one informational note. The bridge contracts deployed for Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain and Elysium were read: all three rewrite every sender, and the address that would be rewritten into each PoolManager has no code and no key anyone is known to hold. The nine OP Stack portals behave the same for ordinary accounts. Their upgraders are another matter: the owner of a portal's admin contract can make the portal emit a deposit from any address, and the OP Stack clients run deposits without a nonce or code check. OP Mainnet governance did this on 31 October 2026 and moved 44.2 million OP out of another address, so the same lever could move every pooled balance without `unlock` (F-140, high, governance; ten networks since cycle 20 added MegaETH). Tempo's interpreter has no path that starts a transaction inside a running one; every caller was listed. The HyperEVM swap check was repeated under a strict rule and widened to the two factories not yet read: 59,225 swaps match to the unit, and about 34,000 of them fail if the 512-bit `mulDiv` branch is made one unit wrong. No endpoint behind the earlier censuses drops logs, and the Monad and BNB Chain censuses were widened with the same result: no transaction ends with a currency still synced. MegaETH's block producer is still closed; 2,254 more of its blocks reproduce in the public validator. The OP Stack chains with ETH gas, Monad, MegaETH, Polygon, BNB Chain and Avalanche match on every rerun. After BNB Chain's fork of 8 December an address that the client routes to its new native token code reverts every call while no token exists there, and no pool can hold funds in such a currency (F-139, info; cycle 20 narrowed the address range).
- Cycle 20 added one finding, at operator level, and closed the questions the cycle 19 critic raised. Exits fit under the gas cap of a mined transaction on all 21 networks: 409 real positions were estimated from their owners and none needs more than 976,067 gas, against a smallest cap of 3,000,000. The exit has no loop, so no other account can make it larger. On the nine networks outside the Arbitrum and OP families no path lets an ordinary account send a call as the PoolManager; MegaETH turned out to use an OP Stack portal, so its upgrader joins F-140. The second namespace in Avalanche account storage has had no public writer since the Banff upgrade, and its keys cannot collide with a pool slot. No earlier clearance rested on an endpoint that answered from the wrong state. Tempo, Arc, the Arbitrum family, HyperEVM, Celo and X Layer match on every rerun. Two severity changes at operator level: F-020 on HyperEVM is now high (the chain's own layer can send transfers in the name of an address a token deployer designates, which needs a validator vote), and X Layer's final release tag lacks a storage trie fix its endpoint already runs (F-141, low).
- Cycle 21 added two findings, both at operator level, and one live example of an older one. A reading of the source that did not start from this review's results found nothing new: no line computes or decides differently from its comments, and the code's safety rests on transient storage, which three new probes at the real PoolManager address confirmed (16 networks for the rule across transactions, 21 for the other two). The compiler release behind the Arbitrum One and Robinhood Chain nodes still carries known faults, and a search of the node source found none of their shapes on an EVM path (F-143, info). Five releases of the integer library under the Rust clients have three faults between them, all off the path the PoolManager uses. On Arc, 1,998 mined native flows through the PoolManager were replayed and none differs. Ethereum, Monad, MegaETH, Polygon, BNB Chain and Avalanche match on every rerun that finished. One public BNB Chain endpoint returns no logs for old blocks without an error (F-142, low). One earlier result was wrong: the cycle 17 hook census had read transaction hashes as pool ids. The recount found a mainnet pool whose hook address has no code, so its creator's own position cannot be removed (F-004, pool creator, about 0.0054 ETH and 4.2 million tCURV).
- Cycle 22 looked at mined blocks instead of simulated calls and added one finding, which changes a gas fee only. A census of PoolManager outflows over about 33 million transactions on five networks found no outflow that pool events, fee collection or a token class on record does not explain; by its own rule it cannot see a wrongly computed amount. Mined traces on HyperEVM (whole history), MegaETH, Monad and Avalanche hold no PoolManager action outside `unlock`, and the PoolManager's native balance matches the traced flows to the wei. The integer library faults cannot be reached from any precompile written in Rust. A failed deposit on an OP Stack chain leaves no transient value behind on any of the six builds with their own handler code. A recount of about 18 million pools found 82 hook addresses without code. One of them has code on five networks and none on four, and on Polygon and Avalanche it freezes dust-sized positions of accounts other than the pool creator (F-004, pool creator). Seven hook addresses are delegated accounts whose holders change the target per network (F-013, pool creator). On Celo, a transaction that pays in a fee currency and refills the slot the fee debit cleared gets the largest gas refund before the Karst fork (F-144, information level); no PoolManager value depends on it. The OP Stack chains, Celo, X Layer and HyperEVM match on every rerun.
- Cycle 23 compared what block producers wrote with the source and added one finding, which needs governance. 163,758 mined PoolManager transactions on ten networks were re-run through a model written from `src/`, each from its own prestate. 3.93 million words were compared (storage, return values, logs, hook calldata) and none differs, and the PoolManager's native balance change matches in every transaction to the wei. A deliberate one-unit change to the model shows up on the real data, though rarely for the tick bitmap and the rounding branches. HyperEVM's official endpoint and two replicas hold the same word in all 8,464 PoolManager slots ever written, and no ordinary account can send a transaction with gas price 0 there. On Monad, tests have now entered 899 of the 913 basic blocks of the compiled PoolManager; the other 14 are unreachable or reverting sides, and every run gave the interpreter's result. Where a stranger can replay a hook's deployment onto an empty address, the stranger gains no role. No fee or refund path of any client lets a wrapped or signed value reach a balance. F-062 on MegaETH is now confirmed at low: one transaction that fills the block's budget for new state delays other users' exits by about one block, with no loss. Nitro carries Amsterdam rules that are switched off, and the PoolManager bytecode has no instruction they would reinterpret (F-145, governance, information level). The Arbitrum family, Tempo, Arc, Monad and MegaETH match on every rerun.
- Cycle 24 worked through the gaps of cycle 23 and added four findings, none of which lets an ordinary account take or freeze funds. The 62,820 transactions traced on Monad, MegaETH, HyperEVM and Base were rerun instruction by instruction: every frame matches, and of the 387 conditional jumps the producers executed both sides of 110, 116 and 121. No producer of the three has run `donate`, `clear`, `settleFor`, an ERC-6909 transfer or approval, or the overflow fallback of `getNextSqrtPriceFromAmount0RoundingUp`, and HyperEVM has never run an exact-output swap. Those paths are tested through `eth_call` and the reference interpreter only (recorded under F-118, F-130 and F-120). 611 failed mined transactions that entered the PoolManager are all source reverts or out of gas, and successful transactions without a PoolManager log replay as well. No client other than Avalanche transforms or shares a storage key, and no cache in front of `KECCAK256` is keyed by less than its input. The word by word replay now covers all 21 networks: about 3.3 million more words on eleven networks, none different. New on record: one Polygon endpoint answers from five unpublished builds (F-146), two minority Ethereum clients select jump destination code by CPU (F-147), a reorganisation after the Lagoon fork can replace dependent blocks on OP Mainnet and Unichain (F-148, operator, low), and a transaction that failed validation halted Base for 116 minutes in June 2026 through a builder fault that is fixed (F-149, any account, information level).
- Cycle 25 ran the paths that the producers of MegaETH, Monad and HyperEVM have never executed, and added three findings, none of which lets an ordinary account take or freeze funds. 1,533 cases went through `eth_call` at the real PoolManager address of all 21 networks: `donate`, `clear`, `settleFor`, every ERC-6909 branch, the 100 percent fee, exact output, both tick clamps, the 512-bit paths of `mulDiv` and the overflow fallback in `SqrtPriceMath`. Every network returns the same words as the reference interpreter, apart from three cases that run out of gas everywhere. Each swap was also recomputed with exact fractions that do not come from `src/`: in all 1,031 cases that return, the pool receives at least and pays at most what the exact relation gives. The overflow fallback lead is closed. A wrapped product cannot pass the test in front of it, and every swap that reaches the fallback reverts at the `int128` casts in `Pool.sol`. No network commits a PoolManager transaction from a speculative run without running it again, and 260 mined blocks with several PoolManager transactions show the committed state equal to the traced one. On Avalanche, `latest` is the last executed block (cycle 24 had it wrong), every accepted transaction runs in order, and 14,741 recent transactions replay to the source. One PoolManager build on the MegaETH testnet and eight on the Monad testnet add almost nothing to what the mainnet producers have executed. Tempo with Arc, the Arbitrum family, Celo with X Layer, and Monad with MegaETH match on every vector and script. New on record: X Layer lets any account reach the PoolManager at zero gas price through two whitelisted contracts, which changes the sender's cost only (F-151, any account, information level); Monad's main branch rewrites its integer library ahead of any release (F-150, operator); and an Avalanche node serves `latest` from its own execution about five seconds before consensus confirms it (F-152, operator).
- Cycle 26 ran the hook return paths and the native value paths, and added two findings, neither of which lets an ordinary account take or freeze funds. 575 cases with a hook double went through `eth_call` at the real PoolManager address of all 21 networks. Every hook case and every native settle case returns the reference words everywhere, and five ownership rules that do not come from `src/` hold on every case. Networks differ only in native takes to addresses that hold a precompile on one network and not on another, which changes the caller's own payout and nothing else. Long sequences of up to 512 swaps with alternating denominators match on every network except Tempo, where the endpoint's gas limit cut three of them short. The `MULMOD` memo in Polygon's client keys on the full modulus and passed a direct test on 1.25 million pairs. On Monad `latest` is a proposed block that has no vote yet (F-153, low, operator level), and from 8 December Tempo validators will vote on a block before its execution is verified (F-154, informational). One result is unresolved: in the local Monad harness, 13 hook cases end differently in Monad's interpreter and in its native code. A fault proven in the harness itself fits them, but they have not been rerun without it. The details are in `OPCODES.md` under "What cycle 26 added".
- Cycle 27 settled the open Monad result and added three findings, all operator level. With the harness fault removed, Monad's interpreter and its native code agree on every PoolManager case: the 13 hook cases, both long sequences and all 2,108 earlier cases, at the release mainnet runs and the one before it. The 13 differences of cycle 26 came from the harness. The Monad validator package was unpacked and read with nothing executed. Its 16 integer routines, run from their own disassembly in a model, give the right result on 321,391 cases. A table of all 774 branch sides over every test set shows 47 that never ran, each unreachable because an earlier line enforces a bound. On no network can an ordinary account act as the PoolManager or as another user; HyperEVM's rule that reads a sender from the signature applies only to the producer's own system transactions. The new findings: MegaETH serves state at `latest` that its sequencer can still drop (F-155, low), the OP Mainnet endpoint answers one batch from two builds (F-156, informational), and from the Lagoon upgrade native pools on OP Mainnet and Unichain depend on interop message validation (F-157, low). The details are in `OPCODES.md` under "What cycle 27 added".
- Cycle 28 added five findings, none of which moves or freezes funds. Monad's native code has now run 898 of the PoolManager's 913 compiled blocks and agrees with the interpreter on all of them; the other 15 are unreachable. The gcc-built `MULMOD` and `MUL` literal folds of Monad's shipped emitter computed the right result on 99,364 cases. Tempo's shipped x86_64 and aarch64 binaries were read without running them, and their arithmetic, shift, comparison and jump destination code gave no mismatch in 826,464 handler runs per architecture. Inside a transaction that several parties share, only a stale `sync` and a held lock cross from one party to the next, and neither credits the party that left it. The official routers on all 21 networks call `sync` before every settle. Two low findings record the reverts an attacker can cause: F-161 for one World Chain contract that pays native value without its own `sync`, and F-162 for a user operation run inside the attacker's `unlock`, which is plausible only because nobody showed that an outsider can read pending operations. F-158, F-159 and F-160 are informational and need governance or an operator. The 30,108 vectors of cycle 27 match on Celo, X Layer, the Arbitrum family, Polygon, BNB Chain, Avalanche and HyperEVM.
- Cycle 29 added one informational finding and moved one out of scope. The shift, `SIGNEXTEND` and `BYTE` literal folds of Monad's gcc-built emitter gave the right word in 1,122,300 runs, and that build's storage, keccak and memory growth routines match the source. Tempo's shipped binaries hash and cache keccak inputs correctly on 7,687 cases per architecture and look up transient storage by the full address and slot. No client of the go-ethereum family holds an arithmetic cache except Polygon's, whose `MULMOD` memo compares the whole modulus and passed 1,845,864 vectors. The integer library those clients share has an unreleased rewrite of division and remainder that no client pins yet (F-163, informational, operator level). All 148 storage sites of the PoolManager's bytecode use a constant slot or a keccak output plus at most 3, so two slots cannot fold into one on Avalanche without about 2^128 hash evaluations. F-162 was rechecked and is now confirmed at operator level: only a whitelisted or chosen bundler sees a user operation before it is included. The borrow scripts and the 23 multi-transaction scenarios of cycle 28 match on Ethereum, the seven OP Stack chains with ETH gas, Monad, MegaETH, Tempo under T14 rules and Arc.

What is still untested is listed in `OPCODES.md`. No item on that list is known to be within an ordinary account's reach. The first is still Monad's shipped validator binary. Its literal folds and host routines are now tested, but the register allocator, the spill path and the stack offset writer of the gcc build have never been run. Consensus limits where a fault could hide: tracing nodes run the interpreter and reject any block whose result differs, so only PoolManager code that no mined transaction has entered is exposed. The second is the storage side of Tempo's shipped binaries: `SLOAD`, `SSTORE`, the transient storage host routine and the journal rollback. The third is the set of forks on 8 December 2026 on Tempo, BNB Chain and Polygon. Tempo and Polygon matter most because each runs a single client, and Polygon's fork has not been rehearsed. Cycle 30 works on the first two, lists the PoolManager code that mined Monad transactions have entered, runs the arithmetic and jump code of the Nitro release binaries in a model, and visits Celo with X Layer, the Arbitrum family, Polygon with BNB Chain and Avalanche, and HyperEVM.

The faulty library release (ruint 1.21.0) runs on none of the 21 networks. Ethereum's reth nodes and Tempo run the first fixed release, which is a month old, and Tempo has no second client. As a by-product of running both, the built bytecode turned out to be the same bytes as the PoolManager deployed on Ethereum, so the on-chain measurements apply to this build directly.

## The question

Can an ordinary account take funds that other users hold in this repository's PoolManager, or stop them from withdrawing, on any of the 21 networks where Uniswap v4 is officially deployed? The attacker has no role: no owner or fee controller key, no token admin key, no sequencer or validator power, no governance vote. The victims are liquidity providers, swappers and holders of ERC-6909 claims who use pools in the normal way.

## Answer so far

No critical or high finding exists for an ordinary attacker, and the planted weakness has not been identified. Twenty-nine cycles have found no property of any of the 21 networks that lets an ordinary account reach funds in pools of ordinary tokens. Every finding that an ordinary account can trigger depends on a particular token, a particular third-party hook, or a recipient the taker chose, and the amounts at stake today are small.

Cycle 7 went back to the OP Stack chains, Tempo and Arc, HyperEVM, Monad and MegaETH, walked the five audit reports in `docs/security/audits` against `src/` finding by finding, and swept every priced currency for transfers that cost the sender more than the amount. The network units found nothing an ordinary account can use. The audit walk found every reported hazard closed in the source or left open by a recorded decision of the team, and two guards that the reports list as fixes and the source does not have, neither of which moves funds. The sweep raised the amount behind one earlier finding: the Arc PoolManager holds about 107,000 USD of a token that charges the sender on top.

Sixty-four findings can be triggered by an ordinary account: 16 medium, 35 low and 13 informational. Six of the informational ones record a mismatch between the source and its comments, tests or audit record, four describe a network rule that moves no funds (F-125 on Arc, F-127 on Base, F-139 on BNB Chain, F-151 on X Layer), one is a gas refund on Celo that changes the fee only (F-144), one is a past halt of Base whose cause is fixed (F-149), and one records a stale branch in the reth execution cache that nothing feeds today (F-133). The findings fall into eleven groups.

Capture of balance growth (F-041, F-066, F-067, F-091 medium; F-060, F-081, F-110 low). Any account can call `sync`, cause the PoolManager's balance of a token to rise, and call `settle` to be credited the rise. For Ampleforth on Ethereum the public rebase is guarded by `msg.sender == tx.origin`, and an EIP-7702 delegated account passes that check while running code. On Polygon the capture is in regular use: since 22 May 2026 six accounts have wrapped each sLGNS rebase in `sync` and `settle` and minted the PoolManager's share as claims, 661 mints for 20,418.69 sLGNS, about 3,300 USD. The same is done with sSAC on BNB Smart Chain, where F-066 had recorded the path as unused: a cycle 6 verifier counted 188 payout transactions for 271.92 sSAC since block 115,890,216. Smaller cases are Interest Protocol's USDI on Ethereum, Benqi rewards on qiAVAX on Avalanche, TITHE on Robinhood Chain, where a public `claimFor` call pays 10,002.56 TITHE of holder rewards to the PoolManager, and a MortgageFi vault share on Base, where the same kind of call adds 2.58 shares. In every case the amount taken is growth that no pool, position or claim was credited with, and principal does not fall. A search of 53.3 million claim mint logs on the 21 networks found mints without a matching payment only on Polygon, in three ids that were already known.

Payments someone addressed to the PoolManager (F-135, F-137 low). This is the same `sync` and `settle` capture applied to a delivery instead of a rebase. From Tempo's T15 fork a failed zone deposit is refunded to the PoolManager inside a portal batch that also runs other accounts' callbacks. On 17 networks a CCTP mint without a destination caller, or a canonical bridge relay that anyone may execute, pays the recipient its sender named inside the executor's transaction. In both cases an account that brackets the delivery takes it. The money belongs to the sender who named the PoolManager and would otherwise sit there unclaimable; pools and claims are not touched.

Tokens that let a third party lock or remove the PoolManager's balance (F-078, F-079, F-085, F-090 medium; F-082, F-095, F-099 low). These reach funds that pools have recorded, which the growth captures above do not.

- Two QANX contracts and seventeen tokens from one generator count locked units in `balanceOf` and let any holder send locked units to another address. An account can take liquid units from the PoolManager, pay back in locked ones between `sync` and `settle`, and leave the pools with units that cannot be sent. A holder of locked QANX gains the liquid units. This works on Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain and Polygon. About 133 USD of current QANX and about 250 USD of generator tokens are exposed, and the 21.2 million old QANX in the Ethereum PoolManager belong to a contract its issuer abandoned in 2022.
- Old ADX and CDT on Ethereum let any holder grant revocable vesting tokens. An account can take X, grant X back to the PoolManager, settle, and then revoke the grant, which removes X with no debit. The verifier reproduced it with `eth_simulateV1`. The PoolManager holds 16.6 ADX and 310 CDT, without a market price.
- DeadToken on Base lets a holder of 1,000,000 DEAD claim the balance of any address that has been idle for 90 days. On 17 November 2026 an ordinary account borrowed that amount and took the PoolManager's whole balance, 14,899.78 DEAD worth about 4 USD, with no PoolManager call. The pools still record it.
- XAMP has a public negative rebase, and 37 unpriced tokens have burn or transfer functions without a caller check. The same call empties any other holder of these tokens.
- Alpaca ibBUSD on BNB Smart Chain has a public `claimFor(address)` that converts a holder's shares into a newer vault token. Called for the PoolManager, it burns the whole pooled balance, 56.66 ibBUSD worth about 56 USD, and stakes the replacement in a contract the PoolManager cannot call. Nobody has sent the call yet. The 16 pools carry fees of 88 to 99.9996 percent, so they may be bait pools.
- Imperium on Polygon lets any account burn the balance of an address that has received nothing for 90 days, and the PoolManager qualifies today. The only liquidity provider of the two pools is the token owner, so no third party funds are at risk. BankToken on Ethereum and a DeadToken copy on Polygon carry the same kind of rule over dust.
- FACTR and TC on BNB Smart Chain join the QANX group. The verifier corrected one point for the whole group: the caller needs no tokens of its own, because the take supplies them, and the effect is a freeze of the pooled units.

The verifier rated the first two high as a class. The review files them as medium, in line with F-041, because the loss is limited to pools of the named tokens. A new deployment has the same exposure once someone creates a pool in such a token and others add liquidity to it. The search behind them ran 389,956 read-only trials over 1,235,609 pooled currencies and found such tokens on these three networks only. Cycle 6 then tested 2,151 priced tokens on 19 networks with every selector in their code, at four block times, and found no token worth more than 1,000 USD at a PoolManager that lets a stranger lower or lock the balance, apart from QANX. That test could not see a charge on an ordinary transfer, which is the next group.

A token that charges the sender on top of the amount (F-092, F-109, medium, Ethereum and Arc). `take` debits the caller by the amount and assumes the token lowers the PoolManager's balance by the same amount. Two priced tokens break that when the recipient is their listed exchange pool. WHITE on Ethereum takes 10 percent from the sender on top, so a `take` of X to its Uniswap v2 pair lowers the PoolManager's balance by 1.1 X. A loop that costs only gas (take to the pair, skim the tokens back to the PoolManager, settle) moves 10 percent of the amount from the pooled balance to the token owner each round and can empty it. The caller gains nothing unless it is the token owner. The PoolManager holds 48.3 million WHITE, about 1,460 USD. ARGUS on Arc charges 1 percent the same way, and the Arc PoolManager holds 46.6 million ARGUS, about 107,000 USD. A seller that routes its sale through `take` leaves the charge to the liquidity providers of the v4 pools, which already record about 188,000 ARGUS more than the PoolManager holds. The deliberate round trip is not free on Arc, because tokens coming back from the pool arrive at 99 percent. The cycle 7 sweep sent a third of the PoolManager's balance of 13,001 priced currencies to 128,687 candidate recipients on the 21 networks, and these two are the only ones above rounding. The cause is the token in both cases.

A transfer cap that one account can use up (F-072, F-083, medium, Polygon). ViciCoin limits every address that its owner has not exempted to 6 VCNT of net outflow per UTC day, and the PoolManager is not exempt. An account that withdraws 6 VCNT at the start of a day makes every later VCNT withdrawal from every v4 pool revert until someone pays VCNT in. Cycle 5 added the consequence for positions. The PositionManager on all 21 networks rejects the `MINT_6909` action, so a removal built by the published SDK reverts as a whole when one currency cannot leave, and the other currency of the position stays locked with it. A hand-built exit that places the blocked side in a new one-sided position works. Of the 15 ViciCoin pools, 8 have a position held through the PositionManager, and the PoolManager holds about 206 USD of VCNT. On the other 20 networks the same lock needs a token issuer to block the PoolManager first.

A ledger shared between pools of one hook (F-084, F-096 medium; F-097, F-098 low). The Klik fee hooks on nine networks keep one pending fee amount per launched token and burn it in the currency of whichever pool of that token swaps next. They accept a pool of the token against any currency from any account. An account that opens a second pool against a currency of its choice can make the hook burn claims that back other tokens' fees. This has happened: an Ethereum transaction burned about 11 USD of claims in a tokenised stock currency, and three pools with liquidity now revert on every swap. Cycle 6 found the gain behind it. The Klik factories record creator fees without a currency and pay them in the token's listed pair, so fees credited in a worthless currency are paid out in the real one. One account used this in four Ethereum transactions and took about 130 USD of other creators' fees. Three route hooks on Arbitrum One pay their exchange from their own token balance whatever currencies the pool names, which puts about 124 USD of the operators' money within reach of any account, and SpawnerHook on Base pays one shared ETH fee bucket to the creator of whichever token is sold next. In all of these the funds sit in the hook or its factory, liquidity providers can still withdraw, and the PoolManager behaves as written. The hook census probed 111 hooks on the 21 networks in cycle 5 and the 20 largest holders per network in cycle 6. The other shared hooks it read, including FeeHookV3, Clanker and Doppler, reject a pool from an ordinary account or keep their ledgers per pool.

Yield that nobody can withdraw (F-012 stETH on Ethereum, medium; F-037 Aave aTokens on Arbitrum One and F-070 receipt tokens on thirteen networks, low). The balance grows outside pool accounting and core has no function that pays the growth out. Principal is untouched.

Payouts to a recipient that swallows them (F-033 medium; F-021, F-047, F-048, F-049 low). On every network family some address accepts a payout from the PoolManager and books it to the PoolManager itself, or holds it: HyperCore bridge addresses on HyperEVM, `L2ToL1MessagePasser` and the ERC-4337 EntryPoints on the OP Stack chains, withdrawal predeploys and fee vaults on Celo and X Layer, precompiles and custody addresses on Arc and Tempo, and Tempo accounts whose receive policy rejects the PoolManager. The caller is debited in full each time, so the only loser is the caller, or the user of a router that passes a recipient through unchecked.

Currency ids that take credit and cannot pay out (F-008, F-077, F-050, F-025, low). Polygon's system token at `0x1010`, the USDC implementation address on Arc, and the Celo predeploy that names itself WETH and wraps CELO. Only someone who pays into such an id loses. One empty pool with `0x1010` as a currency now exists on Polygon.

Delay without loss (F-038, F-039, F-042, F-044, F-062, F-063, F-064, F-071, F-106, low). One cheap transaction on MegaETH uses a block's whole budget for new state. A HyperEVM small block fits about 200 tick crossings in one swap. A HyperCore transfer runs a linked contract ahead of every signed transaction in the block. Any account can fill one tick to the liquidity cap for dust, on every network. On Tempo a first crossing of a tick with a dust position costs about 505,000 gas. In a transaction that several users share, a `sync` left by an earlier user makes a later user's native payment revert if that user's router sends value without its own `sync`. The AGL hook on OP Mainnet and Base has the same shape with a trade quota that one party can leave open for the next. Withdrawals keep working in all of these.

Limits of the design that the audits recorded and the team accepted (F-102, F-104, low, all networks). Any account can set the starting price of a new pool, or move the price of a pool with no liquidity in range at no cost, and core puts no price or amount bound on the first deposit. A provider is exposed only if its add carries no amount bound of its own. A donation goes to whoever holds liquidity in range at that instant, so liquidity added a moment earlier collects part of it. Only the donated amount is at stake.

The remaining six (F-006, F-014, F-054, F-055, F-100, F-101) record places where the source disagrees with its own comments, tests, test doubles or audit record, with no funds involved. The two new ones come from the audit walk. The Trail of Bits and Spearbit reports list a lock on `sync` and on fee collection as a fix, and the source has a check on the synced currency in their place, which skips `address(0)`. Where the native asset has an ERC-20 face, a native fee collection can therefore run between a `sync` of the face and its `settle`, and the account that holds the sync is the only one that pays. The OpenZeppelin report says `noDelegateCall` was added to all relevant external functions, and the source carries it on the four pool functions only. The reports do not say whether the guards were dropped on purpose later.

The other 99 findings need a party the threat model excludes: a token issuer, a chain operator, governance, or the author of a hook or token that the victims chose. They are listed near the end. Five issuer blocks are in force today and an ordinary account can neither set nor lift them.

- On Ethereum the issuers of yUSD, yETH and xSolvBTC have blocked the PoolManager address, and KITE and NTX are paused. About 1.67 million USD held for pools cannot leave (F-080).
- On Celo the GoodDollar owner block-listed the PoolManager on 11 September 2026, which holds about 60,000 USD of G$ (F-056).
- On Arbitrum One the owner of Pleasing USD burned 41,000 PUSD out of the PoolManager and then blacklisted it, which froze the PUSD side of eight pools (F-087).
- On Tempo a TIP-20 issuer has frozen one pooled token (F-065).
- On BNB Smart Chain the restrictor of HOLD has restricted the PoolManager address, which holds 140,775 HOLD worth about 15,600 USD (F-093).

The largest balances under issuer control that are not blocked today:

- Arc: 2.44 million USDC. One blocklist entry for the PoolManager would stop native USDC in both directions, its token face and the bridged WETH token, which reads the same list (F-108).
- Base: 5.32 million OUSD. The block list bound to it is administered by one account without code (F-107).
- Arbitrum One: 1.71 million USDG and 1.07 million Dinari USD+ (F-088). The USD+ index has already been lowered once after the deposit, through a signed storage write, and left the PoolManager 22.25 USD+ short of what the pools record.

One operator hazard is new. The MegaETH portal on L1 rejects a forced transaction above 1,000,000 gas, and any account can spend about 0.003 ETH to raise the storage price of a chosen position's slot above that. The position can then leave only through the sequencer, so a freeze still needs the sequencer to refuse the owner (F-105).

## What has been tested

One balance under two currency ids. Arc (native USDC with 18 decimals and an ERC-20 face with 6), Celo (CELO token), Polygon (`0x1010`), the legacy ETH token on Optimism and Zora, and stHYPE with wstHYPE on HyperEVM, which share one share balance. In each case `sync`, `settle` and `take` were walked across both faces in every order, twice by network researchers and once in a Python model of the settlement code. No order credits one deposit twice. Cycle 3 widened this to every address whose reported balance for the PoolManager could move with another ledger: a sweep of 248 pooled tokens across the 21 networks with zero and dust transfers from the PoolManager address. Wrapped native tokens exchange one for one, and wstETH, sAVAX and qiAVAX mint at their own rate with the caller debited in full. A native credit needs `settle` with value while no token is synced, `settle` rejects value while a token is synced (`src/PoolManager.sol:353-356`), and only one currency can be synced at a time. On Arc the 12 digit truncation gives at most 0.000001 USDC per round against a 20 gwei minimum base fee.

The PoolManager as caller of special addresses. `take` lets anyone make the PoolManager call any address with `transfer(address,uint256)` or with empty calldata, and `initialize` lets anyone name any address as a hook. Cycle 2 ran these call shapes from the PoolManager address against every precompile, predeploy and system contract the researchers could list: all 2,048 predeploy slots on each of the seven OP Stack chains, 122 special addresses on Polygon, BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche, and the full precompile sets of Tempo, Arc, Monad, MegaETH, HyperEVM and the Arbitrum family. Where a call succeeds, the caller owes the full amount, which is the payout-stranding group above.

Opcodes. A verifier deployed nothing and used `eth_call` with init code to run 60 arithmetic, shift, hash, transient storage, `MCOPY` and `PUSH0` vectors on each of the 21 networks, and a second verifier repeated it in cycle 2. All agree with Ethereum. Avalanche differs in one place: the C-Chain clears bit 248 of every storage slot index, so two indexes map to one slot (F-040). The PoolManager writes only to slots 0 to 6 and to hash outputs, so core is unaffected; a hook with hand-picked slots is not.

Transient storage across transactions. Read in client source for the OP Stack (op-revm, Base), Bor, BSC, AvalancheGo, Nitro, X Layer and Arc, and tested with `eth_simulateV1` on six OP Stack chains, Polygon and BNB Smart Chain. A second transaction always starts with an empty map. Batch transactions on Tempo and helper contracts on Arc share transient storage only among one sender's own calls.

A run inside a transaction with its own transient storage. `sync` needs no lock, so a network that let a contract start code under a fresh transient map while keeping its transfers could credit one payment twice. Cycle 3 read the client source of Tempo, Base, Nitro, MegaETH, Arc, Celo, Monad, Avalanche, Bor and BSC and ran two probes on all 21 endpoints: 519 calls to 173 special addresses left a transient slot intact, and every kind of revert restored transient storage together with ordinary state. No network offers such a run to an ordinary contract. The MegaETH keyless deploy sandbox has the right shape and starts only as the first frame of a transaction.

Call value without a balance debit. `settle` credits `msg.value`. On BNB Smart Chain a call from the system address `0xff…fe` carries its stated value and moves no balance (F-058), and nothing an ordinary account sends can run with that caller. Every other value-carrying path that was read debits the sender: OP Stack deposits, Nitro retryables, X Layer gasless transactions, Celo fee currency calls, HyperEVM system transactions, Tempo and Arc.

A chain without a native asset. On Tempo `CALLVALUE` is always 0 and value transfers are rejected, so the native branch of `settle` credits 0.

ERC-6909 operators and spenders. Cycle 5 read 30,347 approval logs on the 21 networks and found 765 addresses with a live operator right or allowance, with about 44,000 USD of priced claims behind them. None was found to let an arbitrary caller move another owner's claims. The Universal Router and the PositionManager hold approvals that do nothing, because their code has no `burn` or `transferFrom` call on the PoolManager. This rests on probes, traces and partial code reading, and several unverified contracts were followed only as far as their `unlock` call.

Forks that are scheduled. Jenner on BNB Smart Chain (8 December 2026) turns some token addresses into native code. Its issuer controls are recorded as F-076, no entry point lets an account without a role change another holder's balance, and a `balanceOf` that runs short of gas cannot return a false zero. Lagoon on OP Mainnet and Unichain (target 11 January 2027) adds interop predeploys. From their source, every call shape the PoolManager can send to them reverts, ETH that a relay forces into the PoolManager credits nobody, and a message relayed to the PoolManager acts only under the messenger's own address. On Base, a token built into the node (B20) takes the direct caller as the source of a transfer and rejects `DELEGATECALL`; that check protects about 5.32 million OUSD in the Base PoolManager and should be read again on every Base release.

The source itself. Ten readings and one audit walk so far: two line by line, a swap trace, a liquidity trace, an attempt to break the custody invariants with a search over call sequences, a check of every mask, shift, cast and `unchecked` block, and two replays of the repository's tests against `src/`. The replays reproduced every concrete vector and revert in the test files on a Python transcription, including 8,920 cases of the v3 comparison run from the bytecode fixture in `test/bin`, and a verifier matched the transcription against 370 quotes and 107 mined swaps on the live networks. Three independent Python transcriptions of the pool math stayed solvent across about 27,000 random operation sequences. The slot constants, error selectors, TickMath and BitMath tables were recomputed from the text. Cycle 6 took the places where the source is looser than its comments and test doubles: `sync` without a lock, `take` on an address without code, the functions without `noDelegateCall`, and the truncation of claim ids. None gives an ordinary account a gain or a freeze. It also listed which test guards each statement that moves funds. No test fails if the unsettled delta check at the end of `unlock` is removed, and with the isolation setting of current Foundry no test pins the lock close or the reset of the synced currency (F-089). The source has all three statements, so this is a gap in the tests.

The transaction filter on Robinhood Chain. The filter precompile holds hashes of single delayed inbox transactions, added by one filterer account a median of 10 blocks before the transaction runs. All 6,234 entries were read. None names the PoolManager or its periphery, and an ordinary account cannot cause an entry that matches other people's PoolManager transactions.

Celo and X Layer at their production tags. The Celo client takes its interpreter from a fork whose instruction files, journal and call frame are identical to the upstream version it names, and its one addition, the transfer precompile, rejects the PoolManager as caller. The X Layer client uses the published interpreter without an X Layer branch. The forks ahead of both (Karst, Lagoon, the second custom gas token design, Glamsterdam on L1) add nothing the PoolManager's call shapes can reach.

Complete currency lists. The Initialize history is now read in full on Base (15.3 million currencies in 15.5 million pools), BNB Smart Chain (78,255), World Chain (826) and X Layer (3,750). The sweeps for locked units, third-party balance changes and shared balances ran on every new currency with a balance on the last three and on 59,170 Base currencies that cover every code family. A census of 17.7 million transactions on the 21 networks flagged 3,752 rows where the PoolManager's balance fell without a matching pool amount, and each was read. What remains unexplained is covered by F-092 and by the tokens that move the balance themselves (F-086, F-094).

The audit reports. Cycle 7 took every finding of the OpenZeppelin, Trail of Bits, Certora, Spearbit and ABDK reports and of the hook permissions note, 113 entries in all, and named the statement in `src/` that closes it today and the network property that statement relies on. The critical OpenZeppelin finding on a native asset with an ERC-20 address is closed by the single synced currency and the value check in `settle` (`src/PoolManager.sol:285-286` and `356`), and a walk on all 21 deployed PoolManagers with the real face on Celo, Arc, Polygon, Optimism and Zora gave no double credit. No fix was found weaker than its report describes apart from the two absent guards above.

The PoolManager as inherited caller. The PoolManager calls code that anyone can supply in three places: the unlock callback, a hook, and the recipient of a native payout. From such a frame, cycle 7 called every natively implemented contract on HyperEVM, Monad and MegaETH with `DELEGATECALL`, `CALLCODE` and `STATICCALL` as well as `CALL`. None acts on the PoolManager's account or accepts a value that no balance backs. The Monad staking precompile rejects every call kind other than `CALL` before it reads the selector, the MegaETH interceptors test the target address, and on HyperEVM the bridge and CoreWriter logs are emitted under the calling contract's address, which HyperCore ignores. Earlier cycles had the same result for the built-in tokens of Base and Tempo, for Arc and for Celo.

The Base client source for built-in tokens. At the release mainnet runs, every balance write of a B20 token needs the owner as caller, an allowance, a token role, or the factory while it creates that same token, and `transfer` credits the named recipient or reverts. Nineteen calls from an ordinary account against OUSD and the policy registry with the PoolManager as holder all reverted or returned zero.

## What is open

The cycle 7 critic put these questions at the head of the queue.

- BNB Smart Chain on the first day after the Jenner fork (8 December 2026, 02:30 UTC). Five open leads wait for it: the call shapes of the PoolManager against a live built-in token, the inherited caller test, and one full round of `sync`, payment, `settle` and `take`.
- Transient storage of the most used hooks. A hook that keeps a value in transient storage without a pool id, or does not reset it, lets one party of a shared transaction affect the next, as the AGL hook does. The ten most used hooks on Base, Unichain, Ethereum, Robinhood Chain, Elysium, Arc and Monad have not been read for this.
- Exits on MegaETH without the sequencer. The verifier found that every removal of one vault contract used 1.17 to 3.24 million gas, more than a forced transaction may carry, which disputes an item the unit had cleared.
- Contracts that call `donate` from a balance they built up earlier on a function open to anyone, where liquidity placed around the call collects the donation.
- One table of every pooled token that an issuer can block, pause or remove at the PoolManager, with the value at stake. Four critics have asked for it and the entries are still spread over a dozen findings.

Three limits apply to everything above. First, the repository's own test suite has not been run: the PoolManager now builds here with the pinned compiler and matches its snapshot, but the tests need forge, which is not installed. The Yul optimizer bug published on 22 October 2026 for solc 0.8.26 was checked against this build in cycle 9 and does not act on it. Second, some node software could not be read: the HyperEVM validator is closed, the Nitro release that Arbitrum One and Robinhood Chain run is not public, and the Elysium build is unidentified. Third, the chain facts were read from the live official deployments, whose bytecode differs from this checkout, and the build of this checkout turned out to be the same bytes, so those facts describe this build as well.

## Findings an ordinary account can trigger

### F-012. Rebasing stETH held by the PoolManager gains and loses balance outside pool accounting

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: stETH balances are recalculated on each Lido oracle report, normally daily, with no transfer. The change is positive on staking rewards and can be negative after slashing or penalties. Lido's integration guide lists Uniswap among applications that are not designed for rebasing tokens and recommends wstETH.

How it meets the code: Pool accounting never reads token balances. Balances are read only in sync and in _settle, and the only credit from a balance is the increase between a sync and the settle that follows in the same transaction (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-364). A positive rebase raises balanceOf(PoolManager) with no sync and settle around it, so the increase belongs to no pool, no position and no claim. The only outflows are take and collectProtocolFees (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57), and both debit an accounted amount, so the surplus cannot be paid out to LPs. Someone who can run sync, the rebase and settle inside one transaction would be credited the whole rebase on the PoolManager's balance. For stETH the rebase happens inside submitReportData, which only an oracle committee member or a SUBMIT_DATA_ROLE holder can call. A negative rebase lowers the balance below the sum of pool and claim accounting, and the last withdrawals of stETH revert in Currency.transfer. Transfer rounding is safe for the PoolManager: a take debits the full amount while stETH moves at most that amount, and settle credits what arrived.

What is at risk: The PoolManager holds 80.4539 stETH at block 26470360. LPs in stETH pools earn no staking yield on it; the yield accumulates as an unclaimable surplus. After a negative rebase the shortfall falls on whoever withdraws stETH last. wstETH (2,295 held) is not affected because its balances do not rebase.

What it takes: A pool must use stETH or another elastic supply token as a currency. The loss case needs a negative Lido report. Capturing the positive rebase needs an oracle committee member acting through a contract or an EIP-7702 delegated account. I derived the rounding argument from the share formula in the Lido guide and did not test it on chain. I did not compute how much of the 80.4539 stETH is surplus.

What can be done: Use wstETH in pools. Interfaces can warn on pools whose currency is stETH or another rebasing token. A hook cannot fix this because the surplus is not reachable through any delta.

Second reviewer's corrections: I raise the severity from low to medium. Low is for hazards with no direct loss path. Here the staking yield on stETH held by the PoolManager is stranded for good, the increase is visible on chain, and the loss is limited to stETH pools and claims, which is the medium definition. The effect is closer to a permanent freeze of the yield than to degrade. The principal shortfall case needs a negative report, which the Lido guide says had not happened when it was written. The same sync, trigger, settle sequence applies to any token whose balance at the PoolManager a third party can raise inside one transaction, which is recorded as a lead.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/lidofinance/docs/main/docs/guides/lido-tokens-integration-guide.md (stETH rebases daily on the oracle report, rebases can be negative, transfers can move 1 to 2 wei less than requested, and Uniswap is named as not designed for rebasing tokens)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/lidofinance/docs/main/docs/contracts/accounting-oracle.md (submitReportData reverts with SenderNotAllowed unless the caller holds SUBMIT_DATA_ROLE or is a consensus member, and the rebase is applied within that transaction)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_call at blocks 26470360 and 26470380: stETH.balanceOf(PoolManager) = 80453883612211151739 and sharesOf = 64372946869317648418; wstETH.balanceOf = 2295.05; AMPL.balanceOf = 16 base units)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ampleforth/ampleforth-contracts/master/contracts/Orchestrator.sol (Ampleforth Orchestrator.rebase() in the project repository requires msg.sender == tx.origin, a check that EIP-7702 delegated accounts can satisfy while running code; I did not confirm that the deployed orchestrator matches this file, and the PoolManager holds only dust AMPL)

### F-033. A take to a recipient whose receive policy rejects the PoolManager succeeds and strands the funds in ReceivePolicyGuard

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: Since T6 any Tempo account can set a receive policy in the TIP-403 registry: a sender list, a token filter and a recovery authority. When a TIP-20 transfer fails the recipient's receive policy, the transfer does not revert. The token credits ReceivePolicyGuard at 0xB10C000000000000000000000000000000000000, records a receipt and returns true. Only the recovery authority captured in the receipt can claim the amount. With recovery authority address(0) the claimer is the originator, which is the from address of the transfer. An ERC-20 transfer on Ethereum either credits the recipient or reverts.

How it meets the code: 1. A user or a router calls take(currency, to, amount) (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The caller's delta is debited and the PoolManager calls transfer(to, amount) on the TIP-20 (src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). 2. The recipient to has a receive policy that rejects the PoolManager as sender, or filters out the token. The TIP-20 precompile moves the amount from the PoolManager to ReceivePolicyGuard with the PoolManager as originator and returns true. 3. The return value is 32 bytes of 1, so the success test at src/types/Currency.sol:64-74 passes and take completes. The recipient's balance is unchanged. 4. If the recipient's recovery authority is its own address or a third party, that party can claim. If it is address(0), only the originator can claim. The originator is the PoolManager, which has no function that calls the guard, so the receipt can never be claimed. 5. PoolManager accounting stays consistent, because the delta was debited and the balance fell by the same amount. The loss is the withdrawing party's.

What is at risk: The amount of each affected take is lost for good when the recipient's recovery authority is address(0), and delayed until a claim otherwise. This hits LP withdrawals and swap outputs sent to accounts that restrict senders, for example custodial deposit accounts with a sender whitelist. Pool reserves and other users are not affected.

What it takes: The recipient must have set a receive policy that rejects the PoolManager address or the token, and must have chosen address(0) as recovery authority for the loss to be permanent. The party that names the recipient in take must not check the policy first. An attacker cannot set a policy for someone else. receivePolicy(PoolManager) is unset on chain, and the PoolManager cannot set one, so payments into the PoolManager are not redirected.

What can be done: Routers and interfaces on Tempo should call validateReceivePolicy(token, PoolManager, recipient) before a take and refuse or warn when it would block. Account owners who use sender whitelists should add the PoolManager address or set themselves as recovery authority. The deployer can document that withdrawals originate from the PoolManager address.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism holds as written. Two points on reach. First, both accounts that reject all senders today name themselves as recovery authority, so they could claim a redirected amount. Three accounts have chosen address(0) as recovery authority, and all three currently allow every sender and every token. I found no account that is in the permanent loss configuration today. Second, a router that takes to itself and then forwards becomes the originator, and a router has no claim function either. The Universal Router and PositionManager on Tempo were not read. The severity stays medium. No third party can trigger the loss, pool reserves are not in reach, and the amount is limited to takes sent to accounts that set such a policy.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`.

Sources:

### F-041. Any account can book a balance increase it triggers at the PoolManager as its own payment, and EIP-7702 removes the origin check that guards Ampleforth's public rebase

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: Mainnet is the reference chain, so the difference is one that arrived after the Dencun rules the build targets. Since Pectra (EIP-7702) an EOA can run code and still satisfy msg.sender == tx.origin. The Ampleforth Orchestrator on mainnet uses that check as its only guard on the public rebase() function, and its source says the guard exists to protect liquidity providers from flash loan attacks. Mainnet also has reward contracts that pay a holder when any third party asks, such as Compound's claimComp(holder).

How it meets the code: sync has no lock check and records balanceOf(PoolManager) for any currency (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288). _settle credits the named recipient with the whole rise in that balance since the sync (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364). Pool accounting never reads balances, so a rise that comes from a rebase or a reward payment belongs to no pool, position or claim until someone settles it. The steps for AMPL are these. The attacker is an EOA that has delegated to its own contract code and sends a transaction to itself. Its code calls unlock, and the PoolManager calls unlockCallback on the same EOA (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). Inside the callback the code calls sync(AMPL), then Orchestrator.rebase(), which passes the origin check because the caller is the transaction sender. When the day's supply change is positive, the AMPL balance of every holder grows, including the PoolManager's. The code then calls settle, which credits the attacker with the growth on the PoolManager's entire AMPL balance, and take(AMPL, attacker, growth), which pays it out (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). All deltas are zero and unlock returns. The attacker paid gas only. The same bracket works for any token where an ordinary account can cause a transfer or a balance increase to the PoolManager: a reward claim on behalf of a holder, an airdrop claim that pays a named account, or a rebase with a public trigger. A negative rebase is never bracketed, so liquidity providers keep the losses and lose the gains. This is the unprivileged form of the stETH finding from cycle 1, where the trigger needs an oracle member.

What is at risk: The gain of each positive rebase or reward payment on the full balance the PoolManager holds in that token, across all pools, claims and accrued protocol fees. The existing mainnet PoolManager holds 16 base units of AMPL, and the public triggers I simulated moved nothing at block 26471590 (0 COMP from claimComp, no sOHM change), so the amount at risk today is close to zero. The new deployment starts empty. The exposure grows with any pool that lists a token of this kind. Without an attacker the same increase stays in the PoolManager as a surplus that core has no function to pay out, so liquidity providers do not receive it in either case; the attacker turns a stranded amount into a taken one.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token whose balanceOf(PoolManager) an ordinary account can raise without paying the same amount: AMPL when a positive rebase is due and the attacker is first in the rebase window, or a token with a public reward or airdrop push to holders. For AMPL the attacker needs an EIP-7702 delegation, which any EOA can set. The rebase window is 1,200 seconds per day (offset 7,200 seconds into each 86,400), and the policy reported inRebaseWindow() = 0 at block 26471631, so my simulated rebase() call reverted. I did not execute the full sequence and did not simulate a delegated EOA; the origin check claim rests on the EIP text and the verified source. I checked 12 rebasing or reward-bearing tokens out of 66,554 token rows the explorer lists for the PoolManager.

What can be done: Liquidity providers and interfaces can avoid or flag pools in elastic supply and reward-pushing tokens and use wrapped, non-rebasing forms. A keeper or a hook can run the same bracket first and pass the proceeds to the pool with donate (src/PoolManager.sol:256-276), which returns the increase to in-range liquidity providers. Token projects cannot rely on msg.sender == tx.origin to keep contracts out. The deployer cannot close this in core without tracking reserves per currency.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right: every step is open to any account, and the pooled token and the pool are not made by the attacker. The assumptions should also list A5, because a take of the pooled balance before the rebase followed by a nominal repayment gives the same gain without a sync before the rebase. A change that only restricted sync would leave that route open. The supports text for the EIP source says the broken invariant is that msg.sender == tx.origin implies an account without code. The EIP words it as the check being true only in the topmost execution frame, and it says the use of the check to ensure that msg.sender is an EOA is unaffected. The conclusion for the Orchestrator guard is the same. The preconditions should say that recent rebases changed supply by zero (block 26467574, and my simulation of the next window), so a gain needs a market rate outside the policy's deviation band as well as a pool that holds AMPL. With 16 base units held, the gain rounds to zero today. The Blockscout row count was 66,563 at my fetch. Severity stays medium on the scale: the loss is limited to pools that hold an elastic supply or reward pushing token, and no such pool holds a material balance today.

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x6fb00a180781e75f87e2b690af0196baa77c7e7c?fields=sources,compilation (verified source of the Orchestrator at 0x6fb00a180781e75f87e2b690af0196baa77c7e7c: rebase() is external, begins with require(msg.sender == tx.origin) and then calls policy.rebase(); the comment says contracts are guarded from calling to avoid flash loan attacks on liquidity providers)

### F-066. Anyone can run the staking rebase of five Olympus forks on Polygon and BNB Smart Chain, and on Polygon bots already book each sLGNS and sATH rebase on the PoolManager balance as ERC-6909 claims

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Polygon, BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: The cause is a token design, and Polygon and BNB Smart Chain are where pools hold such tokens. Staked Longinus (sLGNS), Staked Aegis (sATH) and Staked Klima (sKLIMA) on Polygon, and Staked NovaBank (sNVB) and sShadowsClaw (sSAC) on BNB Smart Chain, raise every holder's balance when their staking contract runs rebase(). That function has no caller check and takes effect once per epoch. F-041 recorded this class on Ethereum for AMPL, where an origin check stands in the way. These five tokens have no such check, and the Polygon PoolManager shows the path in use since May 2026.

How it meets the code: 1. sync has no lock check and stores balanceOf(PoolManager) for any currency (src/PoolManager.sol:279-289). _settle credits the caller with the whole rise of that balance since the sync (src/PoolManager.sol:349-365). Pool accounting never reads balances, so a rise that comes from a rebase is booked as a payment by whoever brackets it. 2. The Longinus staking contract 0x1964ca90474b11ffd08af387b110ba6c96251bfc exposes rebase() to any caller. When epoch.endBlock <= block.number it calls sLGNS.rebase(epoch.distribute, epoch.number), which raises the total supply and lowers the gons per fragment, so every balance grows inside that call (Staking source lines 672 to 693, sLGNS source lines 1068 to 1090 and 1121 to 1123). 3. Observed on chain: transaction 0xfe645530665d1f424807e4ef99fc8cd3569d3fbaab75af4dca893749098eae11 in Polygon block 97,799,112, the block in which epoch 3018 became due, at transaction index 0. An externally owned account calls a contract of 851 bytes. The trace shows unlock, then inside unlockCallback sync(sLGNS), Staking.rebase(), settle() and mint(). The PoolManager emitted an ERC-6909 Transfer from the zero address with the sLGNS id for 9.015568747 sLGNS. The sender paid 3.39 POL at 5,250 gwei to be first in the block. 4. The same gain needs no settle credit: a caller can take the whole pooled balance (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297), call rebase() while holding it, pay the principal back and keep the growth. 5. Read-only tests through Multicall3 with the call list balanceOf(PoolManager), rebase(), balanceOf(PoolManager): KlimaStaking.rebase() raised the PoolManager's sKLIMA balance by 1,276,529 base units at the latest block, with about 1,232 epochs outstanding. The NovaBank StakingV2.rebase() raised its sNVB balance by 82,488,131 base units on BNB Smart Chain. The same call for sLGNS, sATH and sSAC succeeded and changed nothing, because their epoch was not yet due at the time of the call; the sATH and sSAC staking contracts contain the rebase() selector.

What is at risk: sLGNS: the PoolManager held 36,908.56 sLGNS, about 5,900 USD at 0.16 USD, in 267 pools of which 265 have no hook. An epoch is 14,400 blocks and the next rebase adds 8.78 sLGNS on that balance, four times a day. From block 87,258,312 (22 May 2026) to block 97,799,112, 661 ERC-6909 mints with the sLGNS id went to six recipients for 20,418.69 sLGNS in total, about 3,300 USD at today's price, and every one of them sits in a block where an epoch became due. In the one day window the sLGNS balance rose 36.92 beyond its Transfer logs. sATH: 150 mints for 328.86 sATH to two recipients against a balance of 389.04. sKLIMA: 8 mints for 0.0102 sKLIMA against a balance of 247.67. BNB Smart Chain: the PoolManager holds 38,313.74 sNVB and 2,287.62 sSAC; the endpoint returned no claim mint for either id in the last 1,344,000 blocks, and a payout through take would not appear in that search. Who loses: liquidity providers in these pools would have earned this growth by holding the token outside the pool. Inside the PoolManager the growth belongs to no pool, position or claim, and core has no function that pays it to them. The amount taken was therefore never an accounted balance of any user, and principal is not reduced. The balance does not fall back afterwards.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token whose rebase any account can start. The taker must be the first caller after the epoch becomes due. No role, no hook and no token of the taker's making is needed. I did not run the sequence. The call order comes from debug_traceTransaction of one of the 661 transactions; the other 660 were checked by their mint logs and block numbers only. The sLGNS price is the figure the DexScreener API returned for the LGNS and sLGNS pairs. The sATH, sNVB and sSAC staking sources are not on Sourcify and I read their selectors from bytecode.

What can be done: Core cannot tell a rebase from a payment and keeps no total of what is owed per currency, so it cannot compute or redirect the surplus. Liquidity providers should pool a wrapped form with a fixed balance where the project offers one. Interfaces and routers should warn on or hide pools whose currency changes balance without transfers; reconcile.py in the work directory finds them from chain data. A deployer who wants the growth to reach liquidity providers needs a wrapper or a vault held by a hook around the token, outside core.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right: the sender needs no role, no hook and no token of its own, and the pools involved have no hook. Three details need correction. First, the preconditions say the sNVB staking source is not on Sourcify, while the sources list cites the verified StakingV2 record at 0x6bcd1273eba684ff62614e41eff87123113112d1; I fetched it, Sourcify reports a match, and rebase() is public at line 716. Only the sATH staking contract 0x98d21b8e6bf0c1d2dfb0e3fcb82035095dcdd7de and the sSAC staking contract 0x99954eb0b159514d608f64c89a2c5bbe9f42120b were read from bytecode. Second, the sATH epoch is counted in seconds: epoch() returned length 28,800 and an end value of 1795417200, which is a timestamp. Third, balanceOf(PoolManager) for sLGNS was 34,658.405133592 at block 97,804,424, below the 36,908.56 in the finding, so the next rebase adds about 8.24 sLGNS on today's balance. The finding states the scope limit correctly: the cause is the token design and would be the same on any network, the amount taken is unaccounted surplus, and no accounted balance of a liquidity provider or claim holder falls. This is a second and third token for the class in F-041 with live use, and it is unrelated to any difference between Polygon or BNB Smart Chain and Ethereum.
