### F-044. A HyperCore transfer makes the node call a linked contract with 200,000 gas ahead of every signed transaction in the block

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: HyperCore credits a spot transfer to the EVM through a system transaction that calls transfer(recipient, amount) on the contract linked to the token, with the token's system address as sender, gas price 0 and 200,000 gas. The docs state that the linked contract may have arbitrary bytecode and give no guarantee about what its transfer function does. Deploying and linking a spot token is open to anyone; the deploy auction stood at 500 on 2026-11-22. System transactions are placed before all signed transactions of the block and their gas does not count toward the block gas limit. On Ethereum only the block builder decides which code runs first.

How it meets the code: An account deploys a spot token on HyperCore, links it to a contract it wrote and sends the token to the token's system address. The node then calls that contract's transfer function at the top of an EVM block, before any signed transaction. Inside the call the contract is an ordinary caller of the PoolManager. It can call unlock, which calls unlockCallback on it (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114), swap, and settle with tokens or ERC-6909 claims it holds (src/PoolManager.sol:187-227 and 322-336). The PoolManager gives the system address and the linked contract no role, and transient state is cleared after the system transaction like after any other, so nothing carries into the signed transactions. What the account gains is position: it reaches every pool before transactions that paid a priority fee, for the price of one HyperCore action. That is the first leg of a sandwich, or first place for arbitrage after a price move. A swapper loses at most what the price limit and slippage setting of the swap allow. Reserves, positions and claims of other users are not reachable this way.

What is at risk: Swappers in any pool can be traded against from the first position of a block by an account without validator rights. The loss per swap is bounded by the swapper's own limits. LP principal and claims are unaffected.

What it takes: The attacker deploys and links one spot token. The pool action must fit in 200,000 gas; I did not measure a complete swap against that limit. I did not confirm how HyperCore orders several system transactions inside one block, which EVM block follows a given HyperCore action, or whether pending EVM transactions are visible to the attacker in time.

What can be done: Swappers and routers should set tight price limits. Routers should not treat a quote from one block as valid at the top of the next. Pools that need protection from first position trading have to add it in a hook.

Second reviewer's corrections: The finding's own open points stay open: I did not measure whether an unlock with a swap and settlement in claims fits in 200,000 gas, and I did not confirm the order of several system transactions or how early a HyperCore action must be sent to land ahead of a given signed transaction. One addition: the exchange API documents a sendToEvmWithData action that calls coreReceiveWithData on the linked contract with a gasLimit and a data field chosen by the sender, so the 200,000 gas figure may not bound what a linked contract can do at the top of a block. The auction field that reads 500.0 is startGas and endGas. This is an ordering hazard for swappers within their own price limits and is no path to funds held for other users.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:187-227`.

Sources:

- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/hyperevm/hypercore-less-than-greater-than-hyperevm-transfers (Credits are made by a system transaction that calls transfer on the linked contract as the system address; the linked contract may have arbitrary bytecode; a transfer from HyperCore costs 200k gas at the base gas price of the next HyperEVM block)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hl-archive-node/nanoreth/master/src/node/types/mod.rs (Lines 83 to 101: the first system_tx_count transactions of a block are the system transactions)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hl-archive-node/nanoreth/master/src/node/evm/executor.rs (Lines 225 to 227: gas of system transactions is not added to the block total)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_getSystemTxsByBlockNumber for 60 blocks ending near 51,957,680 (Four system transactions from 0x200000000000000000000000000000000000007c called transfer on the linked contract 0x000000000000780555bd0bca3791f89f9542c2d6 with 200,000 gas and gas price 0)
- https://api.hyperliquid.xyz/info spotDeployState (gasAuction currentGas 500.0 on 2026-11-22)

### F-047. A native payout sent to a withdrawal predeploy, a fee vault, an EntryPoint or a Celo core contract is lost to the caller on Celo and X Layer

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Celo, X Layer.

What differs on the network: Both chains have contracts that accept a plain native transfer and book it to msg.sender or keep it. On Celo these are L2ToL1MessagePasser (0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000016), the fee vaults at 0x11, 0x19, 0x1a and 0x1b, the L1BlockNumber predeploy at 0x13, the wrapped native predeploy at 0x06, the three ERC-4337 EntryPoints, and the Celo core contracts FeeHandler, MentoFeeHandlerSeller, UniswapFeeHandlerSeller, Reserve and Governance. On X Layer the fee vaults, 0x13, both Wrapped OKB contracts and the EntryPoints accept value. The X Layer L2ToL1MessagePasser refuses value because the chain runs in custom gas token mode.

How it meets the code: 1. take(address(0), to, amount) debits the caller by amount and makes the PoolManager send amount of the native asset to the recipient with empty calldata (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:44-52). The recipient sees the PoolManager as msg.sender. 2. On Celo, L2ToL1MessagePasser.receive() calls initiateWithdrawal(msg.sender, 100000, empty data). The CELO leaves the L2 and the withdrawal names the PoolManager address as L1 target. That address has no code and no balance on Ethereum. 3. An EntryPoint books the amount as a deposit of the PoolManager. The PoolManager cannot call withdrawTo, and the EntryPoint spends a deposit only after the account answers a validation call that src/ does not implement. 4. A fee vault, the Reserve, the FeeHandler contracts and Governance keep the amount as their own balance. 5. The wrapped native contracts mint wrapped tokens to the PoolManager address. A caller who syncs the wrapper first and settles afterwards is credited that amount under the wrapper's currency id, which is an ordinary wrap at one to one. 6. In every case the caller still owes amount in currency address(0) and must pay it before unlock returns (src/PoolManager.sol:112, 349-364). Pool reserves are unchanged in total. 7. A caller who syncs an EntryPoint, takes native value to it and settles is credited amount under the EntryPoint's currency id, because the EntryPoint's balanceOf is the deposit (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 358-360). The EntryPoint has no transfer function, so that credit can never be taken (src/types/Currency.sol:60-86).

What is at risk: The loss is the amount of one payout and falls on the account that chose the recipient. No pool reserve, no claim of another user and no protocol fee is reduced, and an attacker gains nothing by doing this on purpose.

What it takes: A user, a router or a front end passes one of these addresses as the recipient of a native take. Core gives no third party a way to choose the recipient of another account's payout. Whether the deployed routers and the PositionManager filter recipients was not read.

What can be done: Routers and front ends can refuse the predeploy range, the EntryPoint addresses and the Celo core contract addresses as recipients of native payouts. Users who want CELO on Ethereum should take to their own account and withdraw from there.

Second reviewer's corrections: No change to actor or severity. The actor value unprivileged is acceptable only in the sense that no role is needed. The one party that loses is the account that named the recipient, so this item is no path to other users' funds and no attacker gains from it. Step 7 should add that the credit under the EntryPoint id has to be cleared or minted before unlock returns. The op-stack-eth unit recorded the same hazard for the ETH chains, and the two entries can be merged.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/celo-org/optimism/celo-rebase-17/packages/contracts-bedrock/src/L2/L2ToL1MessagePasser.sol (receive() calls initiateWithdrawal(msg.sender, RECEIVE_DEFAULT_GAS_LIMIT, empty bytes) with a default gas limit of 100,000.)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call with from 0x288dc841A52FCA2707c6947B3A777c5E56cd87BC, empty calldata and value 1 wei and 10^18 wei to each listed address; version() on 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000016 (The value calls succeed for the listed Celo addresses. The message passer reports version 1.1.1-beta.1.)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_call with from 0x360e68faccca8ca495c1b759fd9eee466db9fb32, empty calldata and value to each listed address; version() on 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000016 (The value calls succeed for the fee vaults, 0x13, both Wrapped OKB contracts and the EntryPoints. The message passer reports version 1.2.0+custom-gas-token and reverts with 0xcdfaa111 when value is sent.)
- https://forno.celo.org and https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_call balanceOf(PoolManager) and transfer(address,uint256) on 0x5FF137D4b0FDCD49DcA30c7CF57E578a026d2789, 0x0000000071727De22E5E9d8BAf0edAc6f37da032 and 0x4337084D9E255Ff0702461CF8895CE9E3b5Ff108 (Each EntryPoint answers balanceOf with 0 for the PoolManager and reverts on transfer.)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com eth_getCode and eth_getBalance of 0x288dc841A52FCA2707c6947B3A777c5E56cd87BC and 0x360e68faccca8ca495c1b759fd9eee466db9fb32 (Neither PoolManager address has code or balance on Ethereum.)

### F-048. A native payout sent to L2ToL1MessagePasser, an ERC-4337 EntryPoint or the L1BlockNumber predeploy is booked to the PoolManager's own address and is lost to the caller

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Optimism, Base, Unichain, Zora, Worldchain, Ink, Soneium.

What differs on the network: OP Stack chains have a withdrawal contract, L2ToL1MessagePasser at 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000016, whose receive() starts a withdrawal to L1 for msg.sender and uses msg.sender as the L1 target. Ethereum has no contract with that behaviour. The legacy L1BlockNumber predeploy at 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000013 accepts ETH with any calldata and has no function that pays it out. The ERC-4337 EntryPoint, which also exists on Ethereum, credits a plain ETH transfer as a deposit of msg.sender.

How it meets the code: 1. take(address(0), to, amount) debits the caller by amount and then makes the PoolManager send amount of ETH to the recipient with empty calldata and all gas (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:44-52). The recipient sees the PoolManager as msg.sender. 2. If the recipient is L2ToL1MessagePasser, its receive() calls initiateWithdrawal(msg.sender, 100000, empty data). The ETH is taken off the L2 and a withdrawal is recorded with the PoolManager as sender and the PoolManager's address as the L1 target. The PoolManager addresses of all seven chains have no code on Ethereum. If the withdrawal is proven and finalised on L1, the ETH is delivered to that address, where nothing can move it. The value call returned success in eth_call on OP Mainnet, Base and Unichain. 3. If the recipient is an EntryPoint, receive() calls depositTo(msg.sender) and the amount becomes a deposit of the PoolManager. withdrawTo pays out only the deposit of its own caller, and the PoolManager cannot call it. The EntryPoint spends a deposit only after the account answers validateUserOp or validatePaymasterUserOp, and src/ has neither function, so the deposit cannot be spent. 4. If the recipient is L1BlockNumber, the fallback keeps the ETH and returns the L1 block number. 5. In each case the caller still owes amount in currency address(0) and must pay it with msg.value before unlock returns (src/PoolManager.sol:112, 349-364). Pool reserves are unchanged in total: amount left and amount came back in. The caller has paid for a payout that reached nobody. 6. A caller who first calls sync(EntryPoint), then takes native ETH to the EntryPoint and then calls settle is credited amount under the currency id of the EntryPoint, because balanceOf(PoolManager) on the EntryPoint is the deposit (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 358-360). That credit can be minted as an ERC-6909 claim and can never be paid out, because the EntryPoint has no transfer function and take reverts (src/types/Currency.sol:60-86). The native debt from step 1 remains and must still be paid in full.

What is at risk: The loss is the amount of one payout and falls on the account that chose the recipient. No pool reserve, no other user's claim and no protocol fee is reduced, and an attacker gains nothing by doing this on purpose. One sign that the L1 twin addresses do receive stray value: the Base PoolManager address holds 0.0588 ETH and no code on Ethereum. How that ETH arrived was not traced.

What it takes: A user, a router or a front end passes one of these addresses as the recipient of a native take, for example in the belief that sending ETH to the message passer bridges it to the user's own L1 address. No third party can choose the recipient of someone else's payout through core. Whether the deployed Universal Router or PositionManager filter recipients was not read.

What can be done: Routers and front ends can refuse the predeploy range 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000000 to 0x42000000000000000000000000000000000007ff and the EntryPoint addresses as recipients of native payouts. Users who want ETH on L1 should take to their own account and withdraw from there. Nothing in core can be configured to prevent it.

Second reviewer's corrections: No change to actor or severity. The actor value unprivileged is right in the sense that no role is needed, and the reader should note that the only party who loses is the account that named the recipient, so this finding gives no path to funds of other users. Three additions. First, the value call succeeds on all seven chains, where the finding reports results for OP Mainnet, Base and Unichain only. Second, take(address(0), L2ToL1MessagePasser, 0) also succeeds, creates no debt because a zero delta is skipped (src/PoolManager.sol:369), and still makes the PoolManager start a zero value withdrawal and raise the message nonce. It carries empty data and the PoolManager address as target, which has no code on Ethereum, so it gives no authority on L1. Third, L1BlockNumber is a proxied predeploy, so the statement that no function pays the ETH out holds for the current implementation, and the chain's proxy admin could change that.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/develop/packages/contracts-bedrock/src/L2/L2ToL1MessagePasser.sol (receive() calls initiateWithdrawal(msg.sender, RECEIVE_DEFAULT_GAS_LIMIT, bytes('')) with RECEIVE_DEFAULT_GAS_LIMIT = 100000)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/eth-infinitism/account-abstraction/v0.7.0/contracts/core/StakeManager.sol (EntryPoint receive() calls depositTo(msg.sender), balanceOf(account) returns deposits[account].deposit, and withdrawTo debits deposits[msg.sender])
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/eth-infinitism/account-abstraction/v0.7.0/contracts/core/EntryPoint.sol (the EntryPoint debits a sender or paymaster deposit only in the validation step that calls validateUserOp or validatePaymasterUserOp on that address)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/develop/packages/contracts-bedrock/src/legacy/L1BlockNumber.sol (L1BlockNumber receive() and fallback() are payable and return the L1 block number as one 32 byte word)
- https://mainnet.optimism.io (eth_call with the PoolManager as msg.sender and a separate tx.origin: a 0.001 ETH value call with empty calldata succeeds at L2ToL1MessagePasser, at the EntryPoint v0.6, v0.7 and v0.8, at L1BlockNumber and at the fee vaults, and reverts at L2StandardBridge. The same results were read on Base and Unichain.)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_getCode at the seven L2 PoolManager addresses returns empty code on Ethereum, and eth_getBalance at 0x498581ff718922c3f8e6a244956af099b2652b2b returns 58773641109313318 wei)

### F-049. A payout sent to an EntryPoint, a precompile address or a protocol custody address is lost to the caller on Arc and Tempo

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Arc, Tempo.

What differs on the network: Arc and Tempo carry addresses that accept a payment from the PoolManager and book it to nobody the caller controls. On Arc the three ERC-4337 EntryPoint contracts accept native USDC with empty calldata and record it as a deposit of the sender. Precompile addresses 0x01 to 0x08 and 0x100, the codeless addresses 0x12 and 0x13 and the system log emitter 0xfffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffe accept value, and the USDC face accepts a transfer to the Native Coin Authority address and to its own implementation. On Tempo the FeeManager, StablecoinDEX and TIP20ChannelReserve precompile addresses hold TIP-20 balances for their own bookkeeping and accept a plain transfer without crediting an internal account.

How it meets the code: 1. A caller inside unlock calls take(currency, to, amount) (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The PoolManager debits the caller's delta by amount and then pays: for address(0) with a value call and empty calldata (src/types/Currency.sol:44-52), for a token with transfer(to, amount) (src/types/Currency.sol:55-86). 2. On Arc, if to is an EntryPoint, the EntryPoint credits a deposit to the PoolManager address. The PoolManager can never call withdrawTo and answers no validation call, so the deposit stays there. If to is 0x01, 0x12, the log emitter or the Native Coin Authority address, the USDC sits at an address without a spending path. 3. On Tempo, if to is 0xfeec..., 0xdec0... or 0x4d5050..., the TIP-20 balance of that precompile address rises by amount and no internal balance of any user is credited. I simulated each of the three with 1 unit of OUSD. I did not find a function that returns such a surplus and did not read the three precompiles in full. 4. In every case the caller still owes amount and must settle it before unlock returns (src/PoolManager.sol:112, 349-364). Pool reserves are whole: amount left and amount came back. 5. A caller who first calls sync(EntryPoint) on Arc, then takes native USDC to the EntryPoint and then calls settle is credited amount under the EntryPoint's address as currency id, because EntryPoint.balanceOf(PoolManager) is the deposit (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 358-360). An eth_call of sync(EntryPoint v0.7) on the deployed PoolManager succeeds. The EntryPoint has no transfer(address,uint256), so that credit can become an ERC-6909 claim and can never be taken.

What is at risk: The amount of each such payout is lost to the party that named the recipient. Other users, pool reserves and claims are not affected. The hazard is real for routers or scripts that let a user name any recipient, and for anyone who accepts ERC-6909 claims under the EntryPoint id.

What it takes: The caller, or a router acting for the caller, must name one of these addresses as recipient. No third party can redirect someone else's payout. On Tempo the native variant does not exist because value transfers fail.

What can be done: Routers and front ends on these two chains can refuse recipients in the precompile ranges, the EntryPoint addresses and the Tempo custody precompiles. Users should not accept ERC-6909 claims whose id is one of these addresses.

Second reviewer's corrections: The TIP20Factory address 0x20fc000000000000000000000000000000000000 also accepts a plain OUSD transfer from the PoolManager and its balance rises by the amount, so it belongs in the Tempo list. I tested value calls on 0x01, 0x04, 0x12, 0x100 and the log emitter and did not repeat 0x02, 0x03, 0x05 to 0x08 and 0x13. The actor value needs no change because no role is required. The only party that loses is the caller who names the recipient, or a person who accepts a claim under an EntryPoint id, so this item is no path to funds of other users.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.mainnet.arc.io (eth_call with from set to the PoolManager at blocks 32,582,357 to 32,584,426: empty calldata with 10^12 wei succeeds on 0x01 to 0x08, 0x12, 0x13, 0x100, the log emitter and the three EntryPoints; EntryPoint.balanceOf(PoolManager) returns 0; USDC.transfer of 1 unit to 0x1800...0000, 0xffff...fffe, 0x01, the EntryPoint v0.7 and the USDC implementation returns true)
- https://rpc.mainnet.tempo.xyz (eth_simulateV1 with from set to the PoolManager at block 50,287,321: OUSD.transfer of 1 unit to FeeManager, StablecoinDEX and TIP20ChannelReserve returns true and raises balanceOf of that address by 1 unit while the PoolManager balance falls by 1 unit)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/quickstart/predeployed-contracts.md (addresses and roles of FeeManager, StablecoinDEX and the other Tempo system contracts)

### F-050. The USDC implementation address on Arc mirrors the PoolManager's USDC balance, so a third currency id can be credited and never paid out

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Arc.

What differs on the network: On Arc the USDC proxy at 0x3600000000000000000000000000000000000000 delegates to NativeFiatTokenV2_2 at 0xc6ad664ac6679f4ce74e10e91449c93ec1ae3ca6. The implementation computes balanceOf from the account's native balance divided by 10^12, with no use of its own storage, so a direct call to the implementation returns the same figure as the proxy. Its transfer function calls the Native Coin Authority precompile, which accepts only the proxy address as caller, so a direct transfer on the implementation reverts. An ordinary ERC-20 implementation called directly reports a zero balance.

How it meets the code: 1. sync(X) accepts any address and stores X.balanceOf(PoolManager) (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288). With X set to the implementation address the stored figure is the PoolManager's real USDC balance in 6 decimals, 2,445,327.82 USDC at block 32,584,426. 2. Any USDC that reaches the PoolManager while X is synced, through USDC.transfer on the proxy or through a forced native send, raises X.balanceOf. settle then credits the difference to the caller under currency id X (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364). settle with msg.value reverts in that state (src/PoolManager.sol:356). 3. take(X, to, amount) calls transfer on the implementation (src/types/Currency.sol:55-86). The precompile rejects the call because its caller is the implementation and the take reverts with ERC20TransferFailed. 4. A positive delta in X can be minted as an ERC-6909 claim, cleared, or used as liquidity in a pool that names X as a currency (src/PoolManager.sol:310-336). None of these turns it back into USDC. 5. The same payment cannot also be credited under 0x3600 or address(0): one sync slot exists, a native credit needs that slot to be zero, and each inflow is counted in one settle. The USDC that was paid in stays in the PoolManager and backs no claim under address(0) or 0x3600.

What is at risk: USDC paid in under the implementation's id is stranded for the payer, and claims or pool positions denominated in that id can never be redeemed. Reserves of address(0) and 0x3600 pools are not reduced. The loss falls on whoever pays into that id or accepts claims in it. A proxy upgrade changes the implementation address and the behaviour of the old one.

What it takes: Someone must sync the implementation address and pay USDC before settling, or accept claims or a pool in that id. An attacker cannot make another user's router sync a different currency than the one it pays.

What can be done: Interfaces on Arc should list address(0) and 0x3600 as the only USDC currency ids and warn on pools or claims whose currency is 0xc6ad664ac6679f4ce74e10e91449c93ec1ae3ca6 or any later implementation address.

Second reviewer's corrections: The failed take surfaces as WrappedError with ERC20TransferFailed as its context (src/libraries/CustomRevert.sol:83-119, src/types/Currency.sol:82-86). As with finding 0, the loss falls on the party that pays into this id or accepts a claim or a pool in it. No other user is affected.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/5042/0xc6ad664ac6679f4ce74e10e91449c93ec1ae3ca6?fields=sources,abi,compilation (verified source of NativeFiatTokenV2_2: _balanceOf returns account.balance divided by 10^12 and _transfer calls NATIVE_COIN_AUTHORITY.transfer)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-node/main/crates/precompiles/src/native_coin_authority.rs (transfer, mint and burn revert unless the caller is the native fiat token address)
- https://rpc.mainnet.arc.io (eth_call at block 32,584,426 with from set to the PoolManager: balanceOf(PoolManager) on 0xc6ad664ac6679f4ce74e10e91449c93ec1ae3ca6 returns the proxy's figure, transfer(address,uint256) on it reverts, and eth_call of sync(0xc6ad...) and of sync(0x3600...) on the PoolManager succeeds while the manager is locked)

### F-060. A zero amount transfer of qiAVAX from the Avalanche PoolManager runs Benqi reward accounting for it, and anyone can ask Benqi to pay the accrued QI to the PoolManager

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Avalanche.

What differs on the network: This is the reward bearing token case of the Ethereum finding, found on Avalanche with numbers. The PoolManager holds 2,305.6 qiAVAX, the Benqi market token for AVAX. Benqi pays suppliers in QI and in AVAX through its Comptroller, and claimReward(uint8,address) is public and pays the named holder.

How it meets the code: 1. Every qiAVAX transfer calls Comptroller.transferAllowed, which updates and distributes supplier rewards for the sender and the recipient (Comptroller.sol lines 595 to 608). take(qiAVAX, to, 0) records no delta (src/PoolManager.sol:294, 368-369) and still makes the PoolManager call transfer (src/types/Currency.sol:73), so any account can run this accounting for the PoolManager at no cost. The call used 140,444 gas against about 5,000 for a plain token and changed no balance. 2. rewardAccrued(0, PoolManager) is 14.844 QI and rewardAccrued(1, PoolManager) is 0.1286 AVAX. 3. claimReward(0, PoolManager) can be called by anyone and transfers the accrued QI to the PoolManager when the Comptroller holds enough QI (lines 1234 and 1287 to 1291). 4. A caller who runs sync(QI), then the claim, then settle inside one unlock is credited the QI that arrived and can take it (src/PoolManager.sol:279-297, 349-364). 5. Today the Comptroller holds 0.1096 QI, less than the accrued amount, so the claim pays nothing: in simulation it succeeded and the QI balance of the PoolManager did not change. The AVAX reward cannot be paid at all, because the PoolManager has no receive function, and claimReward(1, PoolManager) reverts.

What is at risk: 14.84 QI today, once the Comptroller is funded again, plus whatever accrues later on the qiAVAX that pools hold. The qiAVAX balance is worth about 54 AVAX at the measured mint rate, so the sums are small. Without a caller the QI would sit in the PoolManager as a surplus that no pool can withdraw, so liquidity providers do not receive it in either case.

What it takes: Pools or claims in qiAVAX or another market token of a lending protocol whose reward claim can be made for a third party. The Benqi Comptroller must hold at least the accrued amount of QI. I did not check other lending forks on other chains.

What can be done: None in core. Liquidity providers in reward bearing market tokens should expect to lose the rewards. A hook could claim and donate them to the pool.

This entry adds to F-041.

Second reviewer's corrections: The zero amount transfer is not needed. claimReward updates the supply index and distributes to the holder by itself before it pays. The impact line about what accrues later needs a number: supplyRewardSpeeds(0, qiAVAX) and supplyRewardSpeeds(1, qiAVAX) both return 1 wei per second, and rewardAccrued rose by 103 wei across my claim simulation, so nothing of size accrues now. The 14.84 QI is a remainder from an earlier reward period. Today the claim pays nothing because the Comptroller holds 0.1096 QI, as the finding says. A caller who tops up the Comptroller himself to make the claim pay gains at most that 0.1096 QI. I compared the public repository file with on-chain behaviour only and did not match it to the deployed implementation bytecode. The actor value unprivileged is right, since the claim and every PoolManager call are open to any account. Severity low stands: the amount is small, the condition for a payout does not hold today, and pool principal is not touched.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-369`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-75`.

Sources:

- https://api.avax.network/ext/bc/C/rpc (rpc eth_call at block 101,112,181 and later on 2026-11-22 with a measuring stub placed at the PoolManager address by state override: qiAVAX.transfer(recipient, 0) returns true, uses 140,444 gas and leaves the qiAVAX and QI balances unchanged; rewardAccrued(0, PoolManager) = 0xce0130c391ab383d; rewardAccrued(1, PoolManager) = 0x01c90be69987eff4; claimReward(0, PoolManager) succeeds with no change in QI.balanceOf(PoolManager); claimReward(1, PoolManager) reverts; QI.balanceOf(Comptroller) = 0.1096 QI; balanceOf(PoolManager) on qiAVAX = 230,561,327,030 with 8 decimals; a value call of 10^12 wei to qiAVAX mints 4,276 units)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Benqi-fi/BENQI-Smart-Contracts/master/lending/Comptroller.sol (transferAllowed distributes supplier rewards for src and dst; claimReward(uint8,address payable) is public; grantRewardInternal pays QI only when the amount does not exceed the Comptroller balance)
- https://api.routescan.io/v2/network/mainnet/evm/43114/etherscan/api?module=account&action=addresstokenbalance&address=0x06380c0e0912312b5150364b9dc4542ba0dbbc85&page=1&offset=100 (token balances of the Avalanche PoolManager, including 2,305.61 qiAVAX, 131.06 sAVAX and 10,974 QI)

### F-062. MegaETH skips every later candidate of a block once one transaction fills the block budget for new state, and the specification leaves the order of reconsideration to the sequencer

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: MegaETH.

What differs on the network: MegaETH caps new state at 1,000 entries per block and per transaction. Once a block reaches a block-level runtime limit, every later candidate is skipped for that block. Ethereum has no per-block cap on new state.

How it meets the code: Unchanged on 2026-11-26. One transaction that creates 1,000 new storage slots brings the block to BLOCK_STATE_GROWTH_LIMIT. BlockLimiter::pre_execution_check (limit.rs line 765 on mega-evm main) then skips every later candidate of that block before execution, a modifyLiquidity, take or burn sent to the PoolManager among them. A skipped transaction gets no receipt and, in the words of the specification, may be reconsidered in a later block. When it runs later it meets the same checks in the PoolManager: unlock, the onlyWhenUnlocked gate and the delta count at the end of unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114), and it pays or receives what the pool state of that later block gives, within the caller's own limits. The PoolManager holds no time-dependent state that a delay of one block could change. The specification gives no rule for the order of reconsideration, so whether N filling transactions close N blocks to a chosen exit or only to the candidates behind each one is the sequencer's choice, and the sequencer is a privileged party.

What is at risk: No funds move and no balance is lost. Swaps, liquidity removals and claim burns of other users can be delayed by about one second per filling transaction. A longer delay depends on the sequencer's unpublished queue rule.

What it takes: The base fee stays near its floor. The sequencer does not filter the sender. The attacker pays for 1,000 new state entries per block it fills.

What can be done: Exits under load can be forced through an L1 deposit. The operator can raise the block state growth limit above the per-transaction limit, price state growth more steeply, or put skipped candidates ahead of later arrivals.

This entry adds to F-042.

Second reviewer's corrections: The skip is at limit.rs lines 881-889 inside pre_execution_check, which starts at line 765; the skip rule in the module comment is at lines 33-38. Delay of more than one block per filler for a chosen exit depends on the unpublished sequencer queue rule (operator), as the finding says. The 22,559,525 gas cost was not re-measured in this cycle.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:332-336`.

Sources:

- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/megaevm/resource-limits (fetched 2026-11-26: two-phase block building, skipped candidates get no receipt and may be reconsidered in a later block, no reconsideration order, per-transaction gas cap is sequencer-configured, TX_STATE_GROWTH_LIMIT and BLOCK_STATE_GROWTH_LIMIT are 1,000)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/main/crates/mega-evm/src/block/limit.rs (byte identical to the cycle 21 copy; pre_execution_check at line 765 and the skip rule at line 36)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/main/crates/mega-evm/src/constants.rs (lines 206 and 211: TX_STATE_GROWTH_LIMIT and BLOCK_STATE_GROWTH_LIMIT are 1000)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/network-upgrades/overview (no upgrade after Rex6 is listed, so nothing scheduled changes the rule)

### F-063. Measured on the deployed contract, one empty bitmap word costs about 5,900 gas in the swap loop, so a small block fits about 500 words and a big block cannot hold a walk across the whole range at tick spacing 1

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: HyperEVM small blocks hold 3,000,000 gas and big blocks 30,000,000 gas, and a sender reaches big blocks only after a HyperCore action sets a flag on its account. In 4,725 sampled blocks 78 were big blocks. F-039 derived the cost of the swap loop from repository snapshots and gave about 1,283 empty words per small block. This entry replaces those estimates with measurements on the deployed PoolManager.

How it meets the code: Pool.swap runs one loop iteration per bitmap word or initialized tick on its way (src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437, src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:85-121). I called the deployed Quoter for 63 swaps of 2^100 input units in hookless pools at block 51,997,409 on an archive node, took the gas of the PoolManager.swap frame from callTracer and counted the bitmap slots and tick slots the frame read with prestateTracer. The 49 swaps without a tick crossing read 11 to 63 words and fit a line of 5,871 gas per word plus 39,059 gas. Two pools with tick spacing 10 read 179 words and used 1,080,157 and 1,080,189 gas, which is 5,816 gas per word. One tick crossing added 10,917 to 31,243 gas and two crossings added 61,692 gas (Pool.sol:413-428 and 602-612). The floor of 2,242 gas per iteration in F-039 counted the bitmap read only. The measured figure includes the price computation and the swap step of each iteration. With these figures a 3,000,000 gas transaction holds at most about 504 empty words or about 95 to 270 crossings. At tick spacing 1 the walk from tick 0 to the price limit is 3,466 words and costs about 20.4 million gas, which fits a big block only. A walk across the whole range at spacing 1 is about 6,932 words and about 40.7 million gas, more than a big block holds, so it must be split into at least two transactions with intermediate price limits. At spacing 10 the walk from tick 0 is 347 words and about 2.08 million gas, which fits a small block when the transaction does little else. The deployed routers make the long walk the default: Universal Router 2.1.2 passed MIN_SQRT_PRICE + 1 or MAX_SQRT_PRICE - 1 in 5 of 5 traced swaps and seven other swap callers did the same in 29 traced swaps. A swap sent through them that uses up the liquidity on its side walks every remaining word (src/libraries/SwapMath.sol:63-75) and runs out of gas in a small block at spacing 1. The three levers of F-039 stay as described there: dust positions on every tick (src/PoolManager.sol:145-184), a swap past the last position, and a pool id initialized at an extreme price by a third party (src/PoolManager.sol:117-142). The measurements were taken on the contract deployed at 0x12D4Fd9C5DeDd00ab8a0bCe2CF0167bbf94b6B1F. The cycle 3 critic notes that this is other bytecode than a build of this checkout, so the figures describe the chain's price for the storage reads and writes that the loop in src performs and may differ by small amounts for a new build.

What is at risk: No funds leave the PoolManager and no position is frozen. Two mined liquidity removals used 60,628 and 60,415 gas in the modifyLiquidity frame, and Pool.modifyLiquidity has no loop (src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-238). The cost falls on swappers, arbitrageurs and contracts that need one atomic swap in the 9 pools with tick spacing 1 and the 31 pools with spacing 10. A swap that crosses more than about 95 freshly written ticks, or more than about 504 empty words, has to go to a big block or be split.

What it takes: A pool with tick spacing 1 or 10. For the dust position lever the attacker pays the gas of one liquidity addition per tick. The victim sends the swap to a small block through a router that passes the extreme price limit, or the swap sits in a contract that cannot split it.

What can be done: Routers and keepers on HyperEVM should pass a price limit near the expected end price and split long walks. Contracts that depend on one atomic swap should use pools with tick spacing 60 or more, where the walk from tick 0 is 58 words and about 380,000 gas. Pool creators can initialize the pool ids they intend to use at the market price.

This entry adds to F-039.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor unprivileged and the severity low are right. Three figures need adjusting and none changes the conclusion. First, 5,871 gas per word is the slope of the short walks. On the long walks at spacing 1 the cost is 5,340 to 5,700 gas per word, so the whole range costs about 38 million gas, where the finding says 40.7 million, one direction from tick 0 about 19 million, and a small block holds about 500 to 550 empty words. Second, more than one caller passes its own price limit: 0xbaf9961ba462abee6d4f86d9509c0bdd15f9a044 and the hook 0x6227f0128935079abc6993f1166dc5d716032aec did so in each of three traced swaps and 0xfe6bace2e548fd06735a4242bbd46a3c842e02b1 in one of three. Third, the profile counts 53 hook addresses, and the logs hold 52 distinct hook addresses plus the zero address. The victims of the dust position and far price levers are swappers in hookless pools with spacing 1 or 10, of which 10 exist today.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:602-612`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:85-121`, `src/libraries/SwapMath.sol:63-75`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-238`, `src/PoolManager.sol:117-142`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.purroofgroup.com debug_traceCall with callTracer and prestateTracer, to Quoter 0x108Bfe38532C98f8Cd8D7Ab49c9dDAa3675a1c5e quoteExactInputSingle, block 0x3196ae1 (Gas of the PoolManager.swap frame and the number of bitmap and tick slots read for 63 swaps in hookless pools: 5,871 gas per empty word, 39,059 gas fixed, 10,917 to 31,243 gas per crossing)
- https://rpc.purroofgroup.com debug_traceTransaction with callTracer for the latest swaps of each Swap event sender (Universal Router 2.1.2 (0xeD270A1Bcdc63cf1356D695e7f40961d4819d6bF) and seven other callers pass sqrtPriceLimitX96 4295128740 or 1461446703485210103287273052203988822378723970341; one caller passes its own limit)
- https://rpc.purroofgroup.com debug_traceTransaction 0xa17080717f (prefix), eth_getLogs for ModifyLiquidity (Two liquidity removals in one mined transaction used 60,628 and 60,415 gas in the modifyLiquidity frame)
- https://rpc.purroofgroup.com eth_getLogs for the Initialize topic from block 45,998,366 (9 pools with tick spacing 1, 31 with spacing 10, 139 with spacing 60 and 165 with spacing 200)
- https://rpc.purroofgroup.com eth_getBlockByNumber for 4,725 blocks (4,647 blocks with gas limit 3,000,000 and 78 with 30,000,000)
- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/hyperevm/dual-block-architecture.md (Fast blocks have a 3M gas limit and slow blocks a 30M gas limit; a sender is directed to big blocks by the evmUserModify action)

### F-064. Dust positions on fresh ticks make each first crossing cost about 505,000 gas on Tempo, so a price raising swap through more than about 58 such ticks cannot fit in one transaction

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: Tempo charges 250,000 gas for a write to an empty storage slot, where Ethereum charges 20,000, and caps a transaction at 30,000,000 gas. Calls to the PoolManager also share a general limit of 30,000,000 gas per block.

How it meets the code: 1. Pool.updateTick copies the global fee growth into a newly initialized tick only when the tick is at or below the current tick. A tick above the current tick starts with both fee growth outside values at zero (src/libraries/Pool.sol:532-538). 2. Any account can initialize ticks above the price by adding positions with a dust amount of liquidity in a pool it does not own. In simulation each such add costs about 540,000 gas when neighbouring positions share ticks. 3. When a swap that raises the price (currency1 in, currency0 out) first crosses such a tick, Pool.crossTick writes global minus outside into both slots (src/libraries/Pool.sol:602-611). With fees accrued in both currencies both writes go from zero to nonzero. 4. I measured this on the pathUSD and USDC.e pool with fee 375 and spacing 4 through eth_simulateV1 with state overrides: 5,819,042 gas for a swap over 11 fresh ticks and 10,865,864 gas for 21, against 267,513 gas for the same swap without crossings. That is 504,684 gas per crossing. With only one nonzero global fee growth the figure is about 254,500. 5. A transaction of 30,000,000 gas therefore holds about 58 first crossings with both slots fresh. A swap that has to move the price further runs out of gas and reverts as a whole. 6. Once a tick has been crossed its slots are nonzero and later crossings are cheap. The account can remove and add its dust positions again to reset ticks above the price to zero. 7. Liquidity removal does not cross ticks. It writes at most the two fee growth inside values of the position from zero to nonzero (src/libraries/Position.sol:76-102), about 500,000 gas, so withdrawals stay possible.

What is at risk: No funds are lost and no withdrawal is blocked. Swappers who buy currency0 in the affected pool pay up to 30,000,000 gas per 58 ticks, which is 0.018 USD at the base fee floor and 0.36 USD at the cap, and a trade that needs a larger price move has to be split with a price limit over several transactions. One such swap fills the general gas limit of its block. On a spacing 1 stable pool 58 ticks are a price move of 0.58 percent. The account that plants the ticks pays about 0.0003 USD per tick at the floor.

What it takes: A pool with fees accrued in both currencies. The planted ticks must lie above the current price and below the price that a later price raising swap reaches. Swaps that lower the price cross ticks whose slots were filled at initialization and are not affected. No role is needed.

What can be done: Routers and integrators should pass a price limit and accept partial fills on Tempo, and should size the gas limit from a simulation of the actual route. Pool creators can use a wider tick spacing, which lowers the number of ticks per price move. Nothing in core needs to change.

Second reviewer's corrections: The pathUSD and USDC.e pool with fee 375 and spacing 4 (id 0x2fafb8d5...297d) is initialized and empty on chain: liquidity, feeGrowthGlobal0X128 and feeGrowthGlobal1X128 all read zero. The measured figures therefore describe a state that the harness built by override, and the finding should say so. The count of 58 crossings ignores the 63/64 rule. The swap runs at least two call frames below the transaction, so the count is a little lower. The statement that price lowering swaps are not affected is too wide. A tick at or below the price that was initialized while a global was zero starts with an empty slot, and a downward crossing writes zero when the global equals the stored value, after which the next upward crossing creates the slot again. The cost figures are upper bounds. The PoolManager account holds 13 storage credits (balanceOf on 0x1060000000000000000000000000000000000000), removals that clear tick or position slots mint more, and settle_storage_credit_refunds returns 245,000 gas per credit at the end of a transaction that created slots in PoolManager storage. The refund lowers the fee and leaves the needed gas limit unchanged. Because the builder admits by gas limit, any earlier transaction of the general lane with a higher tip defers a swap that carries a 30,000,000 limit in that block. This strengthens the degrade effect for large swaps and does not touch withdrawals, whose gas limits are small.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:532-538`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:602-611`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:279-463`, `src/libraries/Position.sol:76-102`, `src/PoolManager.sol:187-227`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/hardfork/src/constants.rs (SSTORE_CREATE_COST is 250,000 gas, the general gas limit is 30,000,000 per block and the transaction gas cap is 30,000,000)
- https://rpc.mainnet.tempo.xyz (eth_simulateV1 at block 50,363,527 with a harness contract and TIP-20 balances placed by state override: gas of the adds alone and of the adds followed by the swap for 0, 10 and 20 dust positions)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/payload/builder/src/lib.rs (transactions that are not payments are deferred once the general gas limit of the block is reached)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/fees/spec-fee.md (base fee floor of 600 million and cap of 12 billion attodollars per gas, general gas limit of 30M per block)

### F-070. Interest bearing receipt tokens and time indexed tokens grow in the PoolManager on thirteen networks and the growth can be neither claimed nor captured

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Optimism, Base, Polygon, BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche, Monad, MegaETH, Ink, Soneium, Celo, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: F-037 recorded four Aave aTokens on Arbitrum One. The sweep finds the same class on eleven more networks and in more protocols: Aave v2 and v3, Spark, Radiant, Seamless, Moola, Neverland, Horizon, DeltaPrime, ExtraFi, Compound III and Wildcat. It also finds tokens with a pure time index: 666, EXPT and EXPT2 on Robinhood Chain and two test tokens on Arbitrum One.

How it meets the code: balanceOf of these tokens is a stored amount times an index that is computed from block.timestamp at read time. Inside one transaction the timestamp is constant, so a sync and a settle in the same transaction see the same index and no caller is credited (src/PoolManager.sol:279-289, 349-365). The growth between transactions stays in the PoolManager balance and belongs to no pool. 666 at 0x73eb4a9db8d8c7539ba5701cf1cdd3452038970b has DAILY_GROWTH 1.33e18, MAX_DAYS 200 and a start time 4.3 days before the read, and the PoolManager balance rose from 1,936,719 to 2,595,372 in 93,084 seconds while one Transfer of 34,778 went out. Two side observations. First, when someone supplies to Aave v3 on behalf of the PoolManager, the aToken logs a Transfer for the supplied amount plus the interest accrued since the PoolManager's last index update, so the logs overstate what arrived; this explains the negative differences on aEthDAI, aEthUSDT, aAvaUSDT, aAstarASTR and aBnbUSDT. Second, Compound III lets anyone call CometRewards.claim for a third party; simulated for the PoolManager on Ethereum, Arbitrum One and Optimism it moved no reward token, with 0.000016 COMP owed on Ethereum and nothing on the other two.

What is at risk: Liquidity providers in these pools forgo the interest or the index growth for as long as the tokens stay pooled. Largest balances: 3,410.66 aEthUSDC, 2,268.28 aEthUSDT and 4,565.90 USPD on Ethereum, 60,244 rzRDNT and 1,504.67 aArbUSDT on Arbitrum One, 24,696 nWMON on Monad, 2,786.51 aBasUSDC on Base, 2,501.34 aASTR on Soneium, 548.71 aBnbUSDT on BNB Smart Chain, 845.05 aOptUSDC on Optimism. 666: the PoolManager holds 2,595,372 tokens, 37.9 percent of the supply, and its balance grows by about a third per day. Principal is not affected and no ordinary account can collect the surplus.

What it takes: A pool in such a token. Nobody has to act. I did not test whether a flash loan premium or another call can move an Aave style index inside one transaction on these networks; F-037 checked that for Arbitrum One only. A caller who paid such a premium would be credited only the PoolManager's share of it, which is below 1 percent of supply for every receipt token found except rzRDNT at 3.8 percent.

What can be done: Pool the wrapped form with a fixed balance, for example the static or wrapped aToken, or accept that the yield is lost. Interfaces should mark these currencies.

This entry adds to F-037.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is acceptable only in the sense that no privilege is needed. No account acts and no account gains, nothing leaves the PoolManager and nothing is frozen, so this is lost yield for providers who pool a rebasing receipt token and it lies outside the goal of the review. The title says the growth can be neither claimed nor captured. That holds for growth that comes from time. For Aave v2 and its forks a flash loan premium moves the index inside a transaction and a bracket would capture the PoolManager's share of it at a net loss to the caller; the finding says this in its preconditions and the title should be read with that limit. The 177 rows were classed by symbol, and a token with a stored index that a public call updates would belong to the class of finding 0. I found none in my sample.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-289`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`.

Sources:

- src/PoolManager.sol:279-289 (sync has no lock check and stores balanceOf(PoolManager) for any currency in transient storage.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:349-365 (_settle credits the recipient with the balance now minus the synced balance, with a checked subtraction, and resets the synced currency.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/classified.json:1 (All 289 rows that did not reconcile on the 21 networks with class, both balances, summed Transfer logs and the second read. 177 rows are lending receipt tokens: Ethereum 65, Arbitrum One 27, Avalanche 15, Polygon 14, Optimism 13, Monad 12, Soneium 6, Ink 6, Celo 5, MegaETH 4, Base 4, BNB Smart Chain 2.)
- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com (eth_call on 666: index 3.4568e18, DAILY_GROWTH 1.33e18, MAX_DAYS 200, startTime 1795033136, totalSupply 6,866,820. Balances at both blocks read on https://robinhood.drpc.org.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/comet_claim_sim.json:1 (Multicall3 simulation of CometRewards.claim(comet, PoolManager, true) on Ethereum, Arbitrum One and Optimism with the reward token balance of the PoolManager before and after.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/aave_targets_8453.json:1 (Base: balances of the PoolManager in the 15 Aave v3 aTokens and 5 other candidates at both blocks; 11 aTokens with a balance that grew without logs.)

### F-071. A sync left earlier in a shared transaction makes a later native payment revert for routers that settle value without their own sync

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Optimism, Base, Unichain, Zora, Worldchain, Ink, Soneium, Celo, X Layer, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Elysium, Polygon, BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche, Monad, MegaETH, Arc, HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: Ethereum has the same code path. The networks differ in how often one transaction carries the operations of several users. Among the most recent transactions that hold a PoolManager Swap event, 342 of 463 on World Chain, 300 of 600 on Soneium and 62 of 600 on Base were ERC-4337 handleOps calls, and bundles with two or three user operations occur on all three. On Ethereum the count was 7 of 600. EntryPoint v0.6 to v0.9, Multicall3 and the public Simple7702Account hold code on all 21 networks, and senders with an EIP-7702 delegation were among the sampled swap senders on 13 of them. Tempo is left out of the chain list because no transaction there can carry native value (cleared in an earlier cycle), so the path has no target.

How it meets the code: 1. sync has no lock check and no caller check. For a token it stores the currency and balanceOf(PoolManager) in two transient slots and returns (src/PoolManager.sol:279-289, src/libraries/CurrencyReserves.sol:27-32). Nothing clears the currency slot when the call ends. Only a later sync or a later ERC-20 settle changes it (src/PoolManager.sol:283, 286, 361). 2. unlock returns with the lock flag false and reverts unless every delta is zero (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). The synced currency and its reserves are therefore the only PoolManager values that one party can leave for the next party inside one transaction. 3. _settle reads the synced currency first. When it is nonzero and msg.value is above zero the call reverts with NonzeroNativeValue (src/PoolManager.sol:349-356). The comment at src/PoolManager.sol:348 tells integrators to call sync before a native settle. 4. The attacker needs one call, sync(X) for any token X, that runs before the victim's operation in the same transaction. Carriers: an earlier user operation in a handleOps bundle, the validation step of any operation in the bundle (all four EntryPoint versions run every validation before the first execution), a pre hook of a CoW order, a call in a relayer or delegated account batch. 5. Test, read only. For recent swaps the original call was replayed at its parent block by eth_call. The sender address received the code of the public Simple7702Account by state override and ran executeBatch of [original call] as control and of [PoolManager.sync(token), original call] as test. Of the replays whose control succeeded, 209 native value replays and 193 token input replays gave the same result, and 9 and 7 reverted in the test only. The revert data was ExecuteError(1, NonzeroNativeValue) in 15 of the 16 cases and a router specific error 0x81ceff30 in one (Elysium, cause not decoded). 6. Contracts that reverted: Ethereum 0x3e8282bd1bfabd23249701e978e9db3df3d7073a (unverified, 187,745 transactions). Soneium LiFiDiamond 0x864b314d4c5a0399368609581d3e8933a63b9232 on routes through EisenDiamond 0x7e36665858d17fd1cbfd4fd464d2a3da49aa3b9d, whose facet 0x0b20601669dbce7e14210e24ca2b748cfb6dfc08 is unverified; EisenDiamond was the swap sender in 18.1 percent of 4,082 recent Soneium Swap events. Avalanche AvaxHookRouterV2 0xc8b2b67a1e286f74648a705617d54a0415fc651a (verified source line 103 calls settle with value and no sync), SwapHelper 0xde9d7290959b6060860b983b32f2d65b2701ebc2 and 0xfe807eb087439ec6e59ed5f93fad011223cb50f9. Arc 0x944fe8becf9a02a3e9378b4cadde1b14ef8d2ed9. BNB Smart Chain 0xdc0e98fd40ccd1c1d7084de9f254d7853846cc6e and 0x95337791024b5f0480b353fbafd2882b4d8e99c8. Monad 0x7db99f225af7a1a21b7b6468ed28f4bc60fbe937. Robinhood Chain 0x29d8d0a5c38716ebb4ae1786a46cf0ab74d480db, the sender of 11.0 percent of 5,624 recent Swap events. Zora 0xdb2e211c43ee4ac4665c44e8ae6723adfd62a07f. Elysium 0xa834e70303322d86e5dae95ee47e9c6a073d9812. 7. By source reading only, without a replay: the verified NodeRouter implementation 0x893f30424f8e6064b1d7284e5415be8413901c0d on World Chain (4.3 percent of sampled swaps there) calls settle with value and no sync at line 1127, as do the verified Bot 0xe41698520e4817b55c1841859a36847d50f0c82f (line 1014) and Router 0xeff6cb8b614999d130e537751ee99724d01aa167 (line 836) on Ethereum. 8. Inside a real bundle: three Base handleOps transactions replayed through eth_simulateV1 with a sync placed before handleOps kept UserOperationEvent.success true and lost their PoolManager swaps (5 to 4, 1 to 0 and 2 to 0 Swap events). The account behind them, with swap sender 0x5c40d83c6361aef9604c3d9a20d8452d496035eb, catches the failed leg and ends without trading. 9. No wrong credit follows from the leftover state. A payer that calls sync itself replaces both slots (src/PoolManager.sol:286). A payer that sends value without sync reverts at line 356. A zero value settle against the leftover credits its own caller with the balance rise since the stale sync, which is the attacker's own donation, or reverts on the checked subtraction at line 360 when the balance fell.

What is at risk: No funds move to the attacker and none are locked. The victim's native input swap reverts and the victim pays the gas of the failed operation. In an ERC-4337 bundle that fee still goes to the bundler. Accounts that catch the failure end the operation without the trade. The attacker can repeat this in every transaction it shares with the victim. Users of the canonical Universal Router, the PositionManager and the 0x Settler are unaffected. The largest exposed public paths found are LI.FI routes through Eisen on Soneium, where half of the sampled swap transactions travel in bundles, the NodeRouter on World Chain, where three quarters do, and launchpad routers on Avalanche.

What it takes: The victim's call and the attacker's sync run in one transaction with no sync and no ERC-20 settle by anyone in between. The victim pays native value through a contract that does not call sync before settle. The attacker holds no role. It needs a place ahead of the victim in the same transaction: its own operation in the same bundle, or a bundle that it submits itself, since handleOps accepts any caller. A sync costs 27,891 to 35,717 gas as a standalone transaction on seven networks by eth_estimateGas, and less inside a bundle, plus the overhead of one user operation. Not confirmed: how often an outsider's operation lands directly ahead of a given victim in the bundles of World Chain, Soneium and Base, and whether the listed unverified contracts are open to the public or are single operator bots.

What can be done: Router authors: call sync(address(0)) immediately before every settle that carries value, as the canonical routers and the 0x Settler do. Smart account users: place a sync(address(0)) call first in the same batch when a route pays native value through another router. Deployer: the state cannot be removed without a code change. A change that clears the synced currency when unlock begins would remove it and would break callers that sync before unlock. Publishing the rule at src/PoolManager.sol:348 for integrators on bundle heavy networks costs nothing.

This entry adds to F-014.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism and the actor stand. Six points need adjusting. 1. Ref: F-014 already records that a stale sync makes a later native settle revert at src/PoolManager.sol:356. This entry extends F-014 with carriers and exposed routers and should carry that id. 2. Soneium: the revert data is ExecuteError(1, 0x9eea691b(bytes)) with 0xb0ec849e inside the inner payload, where the finding quotes ExecuteError(1, 0xb0ec849e). 3. Carriers: the statement that handleOps accepts any caller holds for EntryPoint v0.6, v0.7 and v0.8. The verified v0.9 source requires tx.origin == msg.sender and a caller without code (lines 68 to 75), so on v0.9 a sync cannot be placed in front of handleOps inside one transaction, and the carrier must be an operation inside the bundle. 4. Carriers: a sync made in the validation step works only in a bundle that the attacker submits itself. ERC-7562 treats TSTORE like SSTORE (OP-070) and limits validation to the account's own or associated storage, so a bundler that follows it rejects such an operation. A sync in the execution step of an earlier operation is under no such rule. 5. Preconditions: top level value is not needed. The Monad and Robinhood Chain transactions carry zero value at the top level and still end in NonzeroNativeValue, so routers that pay native value from their own balance are exposed too. 6. Reach: an exposed router or account was shown on 11 of the 20 listed networks (Ethereum, Base, Zora, World Chain by source only, Soneium, Robinhood Chain, Elysium, BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche, Monad, Arc). On the other nine the path exists in the code and no exposed integration was shown. Also: every replay ran against the PoolManagers deployed today, which are other bytecode than a build of this checkout. For this build the finding rests on the source lines, which I read. The position of the attacker ahead of the victim inside a third party bundle remains unconfirmed, as the finding says. The finding is outside the review's stated goal: it takes no funds held by the PoolManager and blocks no withdrawal.

### F-077. One Polygon pool already uses the native token contract 0x1010 as a currency, it holds no liquidity, and the inner frame tests leave the finding unchanged

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Polygon.

What differs on the network: Polygon has a genesis contract at 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000001010 whose balanceOf returns the native POL balance of an account and whose transfer returns false unless msg.value equals the amount. Ethereum has no ERC-20 face of its native asset in the protocol.

How it meets the code: 1. The precondition that someone initializes a pool with 0x1010 has been met once. Pool 0x79d1ed6dc35a3472253c1a5f4b13edfc1481e3b4bd70189c481cbf04b34cbf73 pairs 0x1010 with the token 0x5eb10e40c4969dba42f344eb0cc2cd1a4bb3ad21 since block 70,895,654. It has zero liquidity, the PoolManager holds none of the other token, and no protocol fee accrued under 0x1010. 2. From a forwarding stub at the PoolManager address, called by another account, 0x1010 answered transfer of 0 with true, transfer of 1 with false, balanceOf under STATICCALL with the native balance, and reverted on value calls, on the ten hook selectors and on unlockCallback. This equals the top level results of cycles 1 and 2, so take of a non-zero amount still fails the success test at src/types/Currency.sol:64-74. 3. Among all 6,772 currencies that the PoolManager holds, 0x1010 is the only one whose balanceOf equals the native balance, so no second address measures POL. 4. Walk across the two ids. sync(0x1010) records the native balance. A native settle with value then reverts at src/PoolManager.sol:356. sync(address(0)) clears the synced currency (src/PoolManager.sol:281-283), and a later sync(0x1010) reads the balance again, so the stored reserve is never older than the last native credit. A native take or a native fee collection between sync(0x1010) and settle lowers the balance and makes settle revert at src/PoolManager.sol:360. A credit under 0x1010 therefore still needs POL that reached the PoolManager without a call, and it still cannot be paid out.

What is at risk: Unchanged. POL forced into the PoolManager and booked under 0x1010 cannot leave as that currency. The one existing pool holds nothing. Pools that hold POL under address(0) are not affected.

What it takes: A custom contract must force POL into the PoolManager between sync and settle. The actor loses its own funds. No path takes funds of other users.

What can be done: Interfaces and indexers on Polygon should refuse 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000001010 as a pool currency and should flag the existing pool. The probe of 0x1010 should be repeated after every Bor fork, because forks can replace genesis contract code and recent forks were distributed privately.

This entry adds to F-008.

Second reviewer's corrections: Nothing wrong in the mechanism. The figure 6,772 is the number of pooled currencies with a non-zero balance, out of 7,457 token addresses. The actor value unprivileged is formally right because any account can do every step, and the only party that can lose is the account that forces its own POL in. No user who did nothing unusual is exposed, so this entry is not a path to funds of others.

Sources:

- rpc https://polygon.gateway.tenderly.co eth_getLogs for Initialize with 0x1010 as currency0 or currency1, blocks 60000000 to 97807856 on 2026-11-23 (exactly one pool, initialized in transaction 0xc379c9cb2f0c0b51fd792c8983c2b6ec091f7a23c3d85ba11bd077415886e443 at block 70895654)
- rpc https://polygon-bor-rpc.publicnode.com (bor v2.11.1), eth_call with state override, 2026-11-23, blocks 97807369 to 97808355 (stub sweep of 0x1010 with 16 call shapes; StateView getLiquidity and getSlot0 for the pool; balance census of 7,456 currencies; group test in which moving other tokens did not change the 0x1010 reading)

### F-081. TITHE on Robinhood Chain pays accrued holder rewards to the PoolManager when any account calls claimFor, and 10,002.56 TITHE can be booked through sync and settle today

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: This is a further token for the class recorded for Ethereum, Polygon, BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche, found on Robinhood Chain with an amount that is due now. TITHE 0x3751b9250a90342e47173bd3a7c7de4f297c060a is a dividend paying token (functions magnifiedRewardPerShare, withdrawableRewardOf, excludedFromRewards, claimFor(address[])). The PoolManager is not excluded from rewards and claimFor has no caller check.

How it meets the code: 1. sync, settle and take work as in the earlier findings of this class (src/PoolManager.sol:279-297, 349-365). 2. A caller runs sync(TITHE), then TITHE.claimFor([PoolManager]), then settle inside one unlock. The token transfers the accrued reward to the PoolManager, settle credits the rise to the caller, and take pays it out. 3. Read-only test at block 121,278,927 with a measuring stub at the PoolManager address: claimFor([PoolManager]) called by an ordinary account succeeds and raises balanceOf(PoolManager) by 10,002.5557 TITHE, from 97,123.82 to 107,126.38 after one third of the balance had been moved out for the test. The caller's own balance does not change. The source is not on Sourcify, so the reading of the functions rests on selector names and on this simulation.

What is at risk: 10,002.56 TITHE today, 6.9 percent of the 145,685.74 TITHE that the PoolManager holds, plus whatever accrues later. I found no price for TITHE. Without a caller the reward stays outside pool accounting, so liquidity providers do not receive it in either case. Four sampled tokens on Robinhood Chain share this selector set.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token that pays holder rewards on a call anyone can make. The other tokens with claimFor or similar functions that the sweep tried on the 21 networks changed nothing at the time of the call, which shows only that no amount was due then.

What can be done: None in core. A hook or keeper can run the same bracket and donate the proceeds to the pool (src/PoolManager.sol:256-276).

This entry adds to F-041.

Second reviewer's corrections: Nothing wrong in the mechanism. This is one more token of the class in F-041, F-060, F-066 and F-067 and should be filed as a variant of that class. What is taken is unaccounted surplus, and the token has no price that I found.

### F-082. Any account can lower the PoolManager balance of XAMP through a public negative rebase, and 37 unpriced pooled tokens expose burn or transfer functions without a caller check

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Unichain, Arbitrum One, Polygon, Robinhood Chain, Monad, Arc, HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: Token designs again. XAMP 0xf911a7ec46a2c6fa49193212fe4a2a9b95851c27 on Ethereum has an owner contract 0x8ceb211a7567cf399e1ee01e6974bf4a13b64c04 whose rebase() any account can call and which cuts every balance. The other tokens have burn(address,uint256), burnFrom without an allowance check, or transfer functions that move another holder's balance, open to any caller.

How it meets the code: 1. A balance that falls without a take leaves pools recording more than the PoolManager holds, and the last holders to withdraw cannot be paid (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 pays from one shared balance). A fall between sync and settle makes the subtraction at src/PoolManager.sol:360 revert, so it cannot be turned into credit. 2. XAMP: in a read-only trial at block 26,476,665 an ordinary account called rebase() on the owner contract and balanceOf(PoolManager) fell by 1.0 percent, and the account's own balance fell by the same share. 3. The trial sweep found tokens where a call by an ordinary account lowered the PoolManager balance with no fee involved: 10 on Ethereum (for example directTransfer on 0x1d4214081985ad20aa3ca93a2206ae792635cbec and a public _transfer on 0x28c78083f81818e151d84f1103c1c34598281c55, which moved the amount to the caller, and burnFrom on 0x1a7ebb948e9d2a93f7117c693b35003a929c9d84), 9 on Unichain, 7 on Arbitrum One, 5 on Polygon, 3 on Robinhood Chain and 1 each on Monad, Arc and HyperEVM. Most are test tokens with an open burn(address,uint256).

What is at risk: 647.02 XAMP at the PoolManager, without a price. None of the 37 other tokens has a price at coins.llama.fi. Holders of these tokens can lose them at any address, so the PoolManager adds nothing to the hazard. The entry is recorded so that the class is not searched again.

What it takes: A pool in such a token. The flaw sits in the token and its author chose it or missed it.

What can be done: None in core. Interfaces can flag tokens whose balance a third party can lower.

This entry adds to F-069.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: unprivileged. Any account can call rebase() on the XAMP owner contract and the open burn and transfer functions of the other tokens, and the attacker does not need to be the author of the token, which is what the schema asks for pool-creator. The finding also says XAMP has no price, while coins.llama.fi now returns 0.0009 USD. For PNDX and HPNE the caller receives the tokens of the PoolManager, so the effect for those two is a removal and not only a degradation. Severity low stays: the same call empties any other holder of these tokens, the PoolManager code plays no part, and none of the tokens has a market value that I could find.

- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_call with state override at block 26,476,665: after rebase() on 0x8ceb211a7567cf399e1ee01e6974bf4a13b64c04 from an ordinary account, the XAMP balance at the PoolManager address is 639,906,832,242 against 646,370,537,619 in the baseline trial.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-assigned-balance.work/raw/summary.json:1 (Count of tokens per network in the class lowered after comparison with a baseline trial; details per token in raw/assign_<chainId>.json.gz.)

### F-095. FACTR and TC on BNB Smart Chain join the tokens that count locked units in balanceOf and let any holder assign them to the PoolManager

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: FACTR 0xdefac16715671b7b6aeefe012125f1e19ee4b7d7 has transferAndLock(address,uint256,uint32,uint32,uint16) and TC 0x659049786cb66e4486b8c0e0ccc90a5929a21162 has freezeTo(address,uint256,uint64). Both let a holder send units that the recipient cannot move until a release time, and balanceOf counts them.

How it meets the code: 1. settle credits the rise of balanceOf(PoolManager) since sync (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-365). 2. Trial by eth_call with state overrides: the PoolManager address hands an ordinary account A one third of its balance, A calls the function with the PoolManager as recipient, and the stub then tries to send the full balance and the balance minus the amount. For both tokens the balance rose by the amount, the full transfer was refused and the transfer of the balance minus the amount passed. 3. A caller can therefore book locked units as a payment through sync and settle and take the same number of free units out, as described in F-078. 4. Ten tokens gave this result on the new list: FACTR, TC and eight without a price. The test ran on one token per selector family, and TC stands for a family of 5 tokens.

What is at risk: The PoolManager holds FACTR worth 78 USD and TC worth 1 USD at coins.llama.fi prices. The liquidity providers of those pools can be left with units that cannot be paid out until the lock ends.

What it takes: The caller holds the token. No role is needed.

What can be done: As for F-078: interfaces should hide pools of tokens whose balanceOf counts locked units that third parties can assign.

This entry adds to F-078.

Second reviewer's corrections: The effect is freeze. Nothing is siphoned. The caller hands back as locked units exactly what it took as free units and ends with nothing, and the PoolManager can then pay out less than pools record. The caller needs no tokens of its own, because the take supplies them. The finding's precondition that the caller holds the token is not needed. Whether the FACTR lock ends at the chosen date is unconfirmed.
