- https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org (Trial sweep by eth_call with state overrides on the BNB Smart Chain list, rows of class locked-assign for FACTR and TC.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/006/theme-currency-lists.work/raw/assign_56.json.gz:1 (The stub readings for each trial.)

### F-097. Three route hooks on Arbitrum One pay swaps from their own token balance whatever currencies the pool names, so a pool against a worthless token empties them

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: None. The behaviour does not depend on the network. Three third party hooks on Arbitrum One (CamelotHook proxies 0x9b21e1cdd975e58f714fd1bd5a803198d5520088 and 0x157af7a18e040b9c8dc409c13140873d5bb24088, and GoldenGateFeeHook 0x195b3936afeae0f9ada52473bb34d67466a4c0c8) route swaps to an external exchange and hold working balances. The PoolManager behaves as written.

How it meets the code: 1. The hooks have the flags beforeSwap and beforeSwapReturnDelta and no initialize flag, so PoolManager.initialize accepts any PoolKey that names them from any account (src/PoolManager.sol:117-142, src/libraries/Hooks.sol:178-182). An eth_call of initialize from an ordinary account succeeded for all seven PoolKey variants on each hook. 2. Verified source of the CamelotHook implementation 0x57ea4782fbdb6cd8256aae28075ce3a0e7e13f0f, _beforeSwap lines 71 to 176: for an exact input swap the hook calls poolManager.take(sourceCurrency, hook, amount) where sourceCurrency is the input currency of the PoolKey, then swaps on the Algebra pool 0xB1026b8e7276e7AC75410F1fcbbe21796e8f7526 with hardcoded WETH and USDC. In algebraSwapCallback (lines 178 to 207) it pays that pool with IERC20(WETH).transfer or IERC20(USDC).transfer from its own balance. It then settles the bought amount in the output currency of the PoolKey and returns a delta of plus the input and minus the output. Nothing compares the currencies of the PoolKey with WETH and USDC. 3. In a pool that pairs a token W the attacker issues with USDC, a swap of W for USDC therefore takes W from the PoolManager, sells the hook's own WETH and hands the USDC to the swapper. The hook ends with W. Its deltas net to zero: the take debits it W (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297), the returned delta credits it W and debits it the output (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:287-311, src/PoolManager.sol:216-217), and its settle credits the output (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364). The unlock therefore completes once the swapper has paid W. The mirror pool, WETH against a token W2 with the swap going from W2 to WETH, sells the hook's USDC. 4. Test inside a real unlock, without sending anything: debug_traceCall of the deployed Quoter 0x8928074CA1b241D8Ec02815881c1Af11E8bC5219 from an unrelated address, with a state override that writes slot0 of the foreign pool id in the PoolManager as initialize would. For 0x9b21e1cd, pool (codeless W, USDC), input 1650159984937101: the trace shows take(W, hook, 1650159984937101), WETH.transfer(Algebra pool, 1649829952940113) from the hook, sync(USDC), USDC.transfer(PoolManager, 2840657), settle, and the Quoter returns 2840657. For pool (WETH, codeless W2), input 64130252: USDC.transfer(Algebra pool, 64117425) from the hook and WETH.transfer(PoolManager, 37171745553261727), Quoter returns 37171745553261727. For 0x157af7a1 the same two runs return 14148521 USDC for its 8221214982138125 WETH and 20257073477671002 WETH for its 34923926 USDC. For GoldenGateFeeHook the runs return 3759006195674900 WETH for its 10843 units of WBTC and 1851 units of WBTC for its 659454474487714 WETH, through pool 0x03a3be7ab4aa263d42d63b6cc594f4fb3d3f3951. 5. The exact output branch of CamelotHook takes real WETH or USDC from the PoolManager in the callback (lines 193 and 203) and is credited in the pool currency. In a foreign pool that leaves the hook with an open WETH or USDC debt, so the unlock reverts with CurrencyNotSettled (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). PoolManager funds are not reachable through it.

What is at risk: The token balances of the three hooks: 64.130252 USDC and 0.00165 WETH at 0x9b21e1cd, 34.923926 USDC and 0.00822 WETH at 0x157af7a1, 0.00010843 WBTC and 0.00066 WETH at 0x195b3936, about 124 USD together at block 526824360. The balances belong to the hook operators. No funds held by the PoolManager for other pools are involved, and the pools of these hooks (five, one and one) keep working after the balances are gone because the normal path pays the exchange with the tokens it just took. The amount grows with whatever the operators leave on the hooks.

What it takes: None. Any account can deploy a token, initialize the pool, add no liquidity and swap. The cost is gas. The runs above used a codeless address as the foreign currency, which works for the take because a transfer call to an address without code succeeds (src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). A real run needs a token contract so that the swapper can settle its input. I did not send a transaction. Proxy 0x157af7a1 points at the unverified implementation 0xe43403ad1f3b53d1a59d31756bdb1b28b45631e7 and was judged from its traces, which match the verified one call for call.

What can be done: The PoolManager deployer cannot change this. The hook owner can add a beforeInitialize check or compare the PoolKey currencies with the hardcoded tokens in beforeSwap, and can sweep the balances with the owner only multicall function. Until then the owners should keep no balance on the hooks.

This entry adds to F-084.

Rated medium by the verifier. Filed as low because the balances at risk sit in the hooks themselves, outside the PoolManager, and the parties that lose are the hook operators and not pool users.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The mechanism cites src/PoolManager.sol:216-217 for the hook delta. The lines are 220-226. 2. GoldenGateFeeHook routes only when the PoolKey fee is 0x800000 (source line 263). 3. GoldenGateFeeHook has a shorter route that the finding did not see. When hookData is 20 bytes long the hook uses it as the exchange pool address without comparing it with referencePools (lines 284 to 285), stores it as _activePool (313), and uniswapV3SwapCallback sends WBTC and WETH from the hook balance to that address in the amounts the caller states (365 to 380). A swap in the real pool of the hook with a caller made pool address therefore reaches the same balances. I read this in the source and did not run it. 4. Scope: the balances are the fee float of the hook operators (CamelotHook keeps 0.02 percent through ratio 9998, GoldenGateFeeHook keeps its fee until harvest). The victims are those operators. They are outside the victim class of the threat model, the funds are outside the PoolManager, and the fault does not depend on the network. The actor value is still unprivileged because any account can do every step. 5. The quotes differ from the finding by a few percent because the exchange price moved between the two blocks.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:117-142`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:248-311`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`.

- https://arbitrum.drpc.org (debug_traceCall of Quoter.quoteExactInputSingle with a state override of the pool slot0, from 0x00000000000000000000000000000000c0ffee03: the call sequences and returned amounts quoted in the mechanism for the three hooks.)

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/42161/0x195b3936afeae0f9ada52473bb34d67466a4c0c8 (GoldenGateFeeHook is verified. Its source was fetched and scanned for value moves. The behaviour was established by trace.)

Token prices in the ranking come from the same endpoint per network, pair with the most liquidity, position capped at half that liquidity.)

### F-098. SpawnerHook on Base keeps one pending ETH fee bucket for every spawn token and pays it to the creator of whichever token is sold when the threshold is crossed

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Base.

What differs on the network: None. The behaviour does not depend on the network. SpawnerHook 0xb476c448da4453208df4e371fee26f4e630d3044 is a third party hook with 15,249 pools in the Base census.

How it meets the code: Verified source, _afterSwap lines 234 to 310. The hook charges its fee in the unspecified currency of the swap. For an exact input sale of a spawn token the fee currency is native ETH, and the pending amount is kept in spawnerFeesBalance[feeToken] with feeToken equal to the zero address (line 290), so one number serves all pools. When the number reaches minimumFeeDistribution the hook resets it and calls _distributeFees(spawnToken, creator, total) for the token of the current swap (lines 295 to 297): a quarter goes to the Spawner owner, half buys tokens that are sent to that creator, a quarter is deposited for that creator (lines 320 to 360). Fees taken in a spawn token are converted and stored under that token, so only the ETH side is shared. beforeInitialize accepts any caller for a pool of native ETH against a spawn token (lines 188 to 198), and every swap through a whitelisted router calls spawner.createSpawn(swapper, spawnToken) (line 303), which makes the swapper the creator of a new spawn token, so an account can become a creator and sell its token when the bucket is close to the threshold. I did not read Spawner.createSpawn in full.

What is at risk: ETH fees pending for other tokens, at most the distribution threshold per round. spawnerFeesBalance(address(0)) was 945018262798261 wei (about 1.6 USD) at block 61256529. The hook holds 0.5627 ETH in total. The rest of that balance is not tracked by this ledger and I found no function that pays it out to a caller.

What it takes: The sale must go through a router on the hook whitelist. minimumFeeDistribution is not public and was not read. Not simulated.

What can be done: The hook author can keep the pending ETH per pool id. The PoolManager deployer cannot change this.

This entry adds to F-084.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The fee is ETH in two cases: an exact input sale of the spawn token and an exact output purchase (line 255). The finding names the first only. 2. minimumFeeDistribution is 0.01 ETH, read from slot 3. The finding left it unread. 3. The gain is small and conditional. The account that crosses the threshold receives 75 percent of the bucket (half as tokens bought for it, a quarter as a deposit) and 25 percent goes to the Spawner owner. With B pending from others and f paid by the account itself the net is 0.75 B minus 0.25 f, at most about 0.0075 ETH per round, and it is negative today because B is 0.000945 ETH and f would have to be about 0.009 ETH. Effect degrade describes this better than siphon. 4. Nobody ran the path. I did not find the whitelisted routers and did not simulate a sale, so the profit side rests on the source alone and the evidence for it is inferred. 5. Scope: the ETH is held by the hook, outside the PoolManager, and the behaviour does not depend on the network.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:285-311`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/8453/0xb476c448da4453208df4e371fee26f4e630d3044 (Verified SpawnerHook source: _beforeInitialize (188 to 198), _afterSwap with spawnerFeesBalance[feeToken] (234 to 310), _distributeFees (320 to 360).)
- https://mainnet.base.org/ (Block 61256529: spawnerFeesBalance(address(0)) is 945018262798261, totalCreatorFees() is 26132431268132716624, native balance 562744349885641100 wei. The in unlock test with foreign PoolKeys ends in revert 0xb76b08ae from afterSwap.)

### F-099. Any account can burn the PoolManager's whole Imperium balance on Polygon today through scanAndClean, and two more pooled tokens carry the same kind of rule

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Polygon, Ethereum.

What differs on the network: The cause is a token rule, and Polygon is where the token and its pools are. ImperiumToken (IMP) at 0xf236d2b542eadf2246b307bb1278eafb94fadda6 has a function scanAndClean(address[] targets) without any caller check. For each target that has received no IMP for 90 days it sets the balance to zero, lowers totalSupply by the amount and credits 5 percent to the token owner. The token records lastActivity only for the receiver of a transfer, so an address that only pays out is idle by this rule. This is the class of the DeadToken finding on Base (F-085). It differs in two points: the caller needs no token balance, and nobody but the token owner receives anything, so the caller gains nothing and the pools lose the amount. The same selector search found BankToken (symbol USD.M) at 0x718102f857b91eb573b78c41c654af2db05dd0b1 on Ethereum with reclaimInactive(address), and a second deployment of the DeadToken code at 0x720f29e57cda0a987b7ff37ace02b7021aacbc0c on Polygon. The PoolManager holds dust of those two (800 base units and 1 base unit).

How it meets the code: 1. Core keeps no total per currency. Pools record their reserves in pool state, and the token balance is read only in sync and settle (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-365). A take pays with a plain transfer call and reverts when the token refuses (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-87). When the balance falls without a take, pools record more than the PoolManager holds. 2. ImperiumToken._transfer writes lastActivity[to] = block.timestamp and nothing for the sender (verified source, lines 88 to 101). scanAndClean (lines 202 to 215) checks block.timestamp - lastActivity[target] >= burnInactiveAfter and balanceOf[target] > 0, then sets the balance to zero, subtracts the amount from totalSupply and adds amount * protocolFee / 10000 to the owner. burnInactiveAfter is the constant 90 days (line 18) and protocolFee reads 500. 3. State today: lastActivity(PoolManager) is 1753576517 (27 July 2025), which is 484 days before Polygon block 97,824,929 (timestamp 1795442866). balanceOf(PoolManager) is 3,029.036932838782921101 IMP. 4. Read-only trial at block 97,824,929: with a measuring stub at the PoolManager address (state override) an account without code and without IMP called scanAndClean([PoolManager]). The call succeeded and balanceOf(PoolManager) went from 3,029.036932838782921101 to 0. A plain eth_call of scanAndClean([PoolManager]) from an address with no code and no tokens, without any override, also returns without revert. The same result came back with the block time moved forward by 8 hours, 91 days and 400 days. 5. The PoolManager takes no part in the call. No unlock, sync, settle or take is involved, so nothing in core can see or stop it. 6. The balance does not come back. Later payments into the PoolManager restart the timer for 90 days and are recorded by pools as well. 7. BankToken.reclaimInactive(account) has no caller check either. When the owner set autoAction to 1 it burns the whole balance of an account that is past INACTIVITY_PERIOD, and with 2 it sends the balance to the treasury or the owner. In the trial with the block time moved forward by 91 days and by 400 days the PoolManager balance of 800 base units fell to 0, and it stayed at the current time and at 8 hours. 8. The second DeadToken deployment answers getTimeUntilClaimable(PoolManager) with 0 and whiteListedPool(PoolManager) with 0, so the PoolManager is a valid target there now. Its balance has been 1 base unit at every sample since Polygon block 79,980,384. I found no verified source for this address and identified it by its selectors (claimIdleTokens, getTimeUntilClaimable, lastTransferTime).

What is at risk: Liquidity providers and claim holders of the IMP pools lose the pooled IMP: 3,029.04 IMP, of which 2,877.59 vanish and 151.45 go to the token owner 0xe77e6c411f2ee01f1cfbcccb1c418c80f1a534fe. The Initialize logs read in cycle 4 show two pools with IMP as a currency. GeckoTerminal reports a total reserve of about 1.57 USD for the token and no price, and coins.llama.fi and DexScreener have no entry, so the amount at stake today is negligible. The loss stays inside currency IMP because deltas are kept per currency (src/PoolManager.sol:368-378). The finding matters as a pattern: a pool in any token with such a rule can be emptied by an account that holds nothing, and the PoolManager is idle by a receiver based timer whenever a pool sees no inflow for the period. The step 2 trials found no token with a market price above 1,000 USD at a PoolManager that has such a rule, on the lists available (see the leads for the networks where the list is incomplete).

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token that lets any caller remove the balance of an idle holder. For IMP: no IMP received by the PoolManager for 90 days, which holds since 25 October 2025. No role, hook, token balance or attacker made token is needed. I did not send any transaction and did not read the fee and hook settings of the two pools.

What can be done: None in core. The token owner cannot exempt an address from scanAndClean in this source (excludeFromFee covers the fee only). Liquidity providers should leave IMP pools. Interfaces and routers should refuse or flag currencies whose code holds scanAndClean, reclaimInactive, sweepInactive or claimIdleTokens selectors, and a deployment that lists tokens should test each token with a call from a stranger at a shifted block time before it is shown to users.

This entry adds to F-085.

Rated medium by the verifier. Filed as low because the verifier found that the only liquidity provider of the two Imperium pools is the token owner and that the other side of the pools is worth less than one cent, so no third party funds are at risk today.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right for the mechanism: the caller needs no role, no token balance and no contract, and both pools have no hook. The impact text needs three corrections. First, no claim holder exists. The PoolManager has emitted no ERC-6909 Transfer log with id IMP, and protocolFeesAccrued for IMP is 0. Second, the only liquidity provider is the token owner. Each pool has one ModifyLiquidity log, made through the PositionManager 0x1ec2ebf4f37e7363fdfe3551602425af0b3ceef9 with IMP paid in by 0xe77e6c411f2ee01f1cfbcccb1c418c80f1a534fe, and ownerOf for position tokens 33811 and 33815 returns that address today. The party that loses the pooled IMP is the party that wrote the rule and that receives the 5 percent. No third party funds are at risk in these two pools today. Third, the other side of the pools is about 0.052 POL and 396 base units of USDT, so the value that a failed removal could strand is below one cent. The PoolManager received IMP in four transfers (155.9987, 2861.8602, 10 and 1.1781 IMP in blocks 74,452,496 to 74,452,887) and has never paid IMP out. For BankToken the finding leaves out that autoAction reads 2, so the 800 base units would go to the treasury, which is the owner, and that the period is 365 days: the rule first applies to the PoolManager on about 26 December 2026 unless it sends or receives the token before then. For the DeadToken copy the caller needs a DEAD balance, as on Base; the threshold was not read because the source is not verified. The finding is of the class of F-085 and adds no network property: the cause is the token in all three cases, so it does not answer the question of a weakness that follows from how a network differs from Ethereum. I kept medium in line with F-085 and F-079 (loss limited to pools of particular tokens, negligible amounts today). I could not read the two liquidity transactions by hash on the public endpoints, so the sender of those transactions rests on the Transfer logs and on ownerOf.

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x718102f857b91eb573b78c41c654af2db05dd0b1?fields=sources,compilation,proxyResolution (Verified source BankToken: reclaimInactive(address) is external without a caller check and burns or moves the whole balance of an inactive account.)
- https://polygon.gateway.tenderly.co (Archive eth_call: balanceOf(PoolManager) of 0x720f29e57cda0a987b7ff37ace02b7021aacbc0c is 1 at every sampled block from 79,980,384 to 97,824,567; getTimeUntilClaimable(PoolManager) 0 and whiteListedPool(PoolManager) 0 at the latest block.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:349-365 (_settle reads the token balance and credits the rise since sync. No other place compares pool records with the balance.)
- src/types/Currency.sol:60-87 (A take is a plain transfer call that reverts when the token refuses, which is what happens once the balance is gone.)

### F-102. Any account can set the starting price of a pool or move the price of an empty pool for free, and core takes no price or amount bound on the first deposit (OpenZeppelin M-03, acknowledged and not resolved)

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None in the code path, which is the same on Ethereum. Networks differ in who can see a pending transaction, and I fetched no source on that for the 21 networks in this session. The variant that initializes a predictable PoolKey ahead of its creator needs no view of pending transactions.

How it meets the code: 1. initialize accepts any caller and stores the caller's price (src/PoolManager.sol:117-134, src/libraries/Pool.sol:100-107). The price is not part of the pool id (src/types/PoolId.sol:11-15), and a second initialize reverts (Pool.sol:101). Whoever is first sets the price. 2. swap has no check for liquidity. With zero liquidity in range each step returns zero amounts and moves the price to the next tick or to the limit (Pool.sol:344-437, src/libraries/SwapMath.sol:63-106), so any account can move the price of a pool without in range liquidity to any value inside the bounds for a zero delta. 3. modifyLiquidity computes the two amounts from the stored price (Pool.sol:206-236) and accepts no minimum or maximum from the caller (src/PoolManager.sol:145-184). 4. A provider that adds liquidity after the price was set away from the market deposits at that price, and the attacker then swaps against the position at the off market price. 5. Nothing in src closes this. The report records the team's answer that peripheral contracts add slippage protection. I did not read the periphery. 6. On all 21 deployed PoolManagers an eth_call of initialize from an arbitrary account for an unused PoolKey returned ticks 0, 138162, -887272 and 887271 for four prices of my choice.

What is at risk: The first liquidity provider of a pool who adds through code without amount bounds can lose part of the deposit, up to most of its value, to the account that set the price. Pools that already hold in range liquidity are not affected, because moving their price costs the swap amounts.

What it takes: A pool that is not yet initialized or has no in range liquidity, and a provider that adds liquidity without checking the pool price or bounding the amounts. The attacker needs no role.

What can be done: Initialize and add liquidity in one transaction with amount bounds, as the report's update says the periphery does. Integrations that call modifyLiquidity themselves should read slot0 and compare the price before they add.

Second reviewer's corrections: The price moves for free only while the path crosses no tick that brings liquidity into range. Once a crossing adds liquidity (Pool.sol:413-428) the swap costs the usual amounts. The loss needs a provider whose add carries no binding amount bound, and no such path was identified, so the siphon effect is conditional and low is the right level until the lead on the deployed PositionManager is answered. The code path is the same on every network, as the finding says.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:117-142`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:100-107`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437`.

Sources:

- docs/security/audits/OpenZeppelin_audit_core.pdf:13 (Pages 13 and 14, M-03: front-running initialization or the first deposit, the five step scenario, and the update that the issue is acknowledged and not resolved)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:344 (The swap loop has no liquidity check and a step with zero liquidity moves the price with zero amounts)
- src/PoolManager.sol:117 (initialize has no caller check and the price is an argument)
- eth_call of initialize from an arbitrary account on the 21 rpc URLs of research/network-risk/state/networks.json on 2026-11-23, script init.py, results raw/init.json in the work directory of this unit (A fresh pool can be initialized by any account at the lowest, the highest and two other prices on every deployed PoolManager)

### F-104. A donation is shared by whoever holds in-range liquidity at the instant of the call, and core has no statement that keeps liquidity added moments earlier from collecting it

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None in the code path, which is the same on every network. What differs per network is how easily an ordinary account can place its own calls directly around another party's donation: inside one transaction when the donation can be triggered by any caller, or in neighbouring transactions where pending transactions are visible and ordering can be bought. This unit did not establish the ordering rules of the 21 networks and names that as an open lead.

How it meets the code: 1. donate(key, amount0, amount1) debits the caller and raises feeGrowthGlobal by amount * 2^128 / liquidity, where liquidity is the pool's active liquidity at slot0.tick when the call runs (src/PoolManager.sol:256-276, src/libraries/Pool.sol:466-480). There is no minimum holding time, no snapshot and no vesting. 2. A position added before the call earns its share liquidity / total through the ordinary fee growth path when it is next touched (Pool.sol:184-192, Position.sol:92-97) and can be removed right after. Adding and removing liquidity is open to any account and costs only the rounding of the amounts. 3. A second effect makes the set of recipients movable. When a zeroForOne swap ends exactly on the price of an initialized tick n, the tick is crossed and slot0.tick becomes n - 1 while the price is that of n (Pool.sol:413-432). A swap with sqrtPriceLimitX96 equal to the price of tick n does this on purpose. A donation made in that state goes to positions that cover tick n - 1, and positions whose lower bound is n receive nothing. 4. Spearbit 5.1.1 and Certora M-02 describe these two effects, both at medium. The recorded responses are comments (IPoolManager.sol:150-155, Pool.sol:409-412, Pool.sol:80-82). No statement in src closes either one. 5. Read-only log queries show donate in use: 108 Donate events in 5,000 blocks on Ethereum, 138 in 100,000 blocks on Robinhood Chain, 304 on Elysium and 7 on Arbitrum One. In the four transactions I opened, one per network, the donor is a contract and the same transaction also swaps through the pool that receives the donation.

What is at risk: The donor's funds reach other accounts than the liquidity providers they were meant for, and the long standing providers of that pool lose the share. The amount at risk is one donation at a time. The sampled donations are small: 8.0e12 units of currency0 on Ethereum, 11,230 units of currency1 on Arbitrum One, 5.11e18 units of an 18 decimal currency on Robinhood Chain. A donation that is funded by the swap that triggers it gives a caller who triggers it only its own money back, so the loss arises where the donated amount was accrued earlier or where another party's swap can be bracketed. I did not find a donor contract that pays out an earlier accrued balance on a public call, and I did not look for one beyond the four samples.

What it takes: Some party donates to a pool. The attacker holds or can borrow both currencies for the length of the capture and can run its liquidity change directly before the donation: in the same transaction when the donating call is open to any caller, otherwise through transaction ordering on that network.

What can be done: Donors should not donate amounts that are worth capturing in one call. A hook that distributes fees can pay them over time or require that liquidity was present before the block of the donation, and can check both slot0.tick and the price before donating, as the comment at Pool.sol:409-412 advises. Core could not close this without storing per position time data, which the reports do not ask for.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged stands for the mechanism: adding and removing liquidity around a call needs no role. Four points need correction or a limit. First, the effect is narrower than siphon suggests. The only funds that move are the donated amounts, which the donor gives up at PoolManager.sol:270. Principal and earlier fees of other providers cannot be reached through this path. Second, in all three receipts that I read the donation is made by a contract inside a transaction that also swaps through the same pool, so a caller who triggers it funds it. Capture then needs ordering around another user's swap, which is the same position a just in time provider has toward ordinary swap fees. The finding says this and I agree that it keeps the rating at low. Spearbit rates the mechanism medium, and medium would apply under SCHEMA.md once a donor is found that pays out a balance accrued earlier on a call open to anyone. None is known. Third, the remark on senders is slightly off: the most frequent Ethereum sender in the window is 0x3f8262ed65fdd4d46c0d008a49e938769bb22488 with 15 events, whose address ends in 488, and the next five end in 888. Fourth, the unit's raw file has no log count for Base, World Chain, X Layer, Polygon, BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche and Monad, because those queries failed, so four networks with donations is a lower bound. I queried second endpoints: BNB Smart Chain shows 22 Donate events in blocks 135,322,108 to 135,327,108 (13 from 0x8c7852bb503b4579c28a6fa2bcf112c7657a6488), and Base (61,353,982 to 61,358,982), Polygon (97,832,805 to 97,837,805), Avalanche (101,187,597 to 101,192,597), X Layer (1,000 blocks) and Monad (1,000 blocks) show none in those short windows. World Chain could not be queried. The hazard does not follow from any network difference, so it is not the planted weakness the review is looking for.

### F-106. An AGL quota left open by one party of a shared transaction makes the next AGL trade in that transaction revert

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Optimism, Base.

What differs on the network: No chain rule is involved beyond the fact that carriers such as ERC-4337 bundles put the calls of several users into one transaction with one transient storage map. AGL 0x0000000000109de411eabc9f04906d79f06fda0e and its hook PoolAGL 0x0000000456b76bc801253ef2366664750f602840 hold code on OP Mainnet and Base (15,125 and 8,730 bytes), and cota() reads 0 outside a transaction.

How it meets the code: PoolAGL keeps one signed quota in a transient slot. afterSwap adds the negated AGL amount of the swapper to it. The token calls consumirSaida when AGL leaves the PoolManager, which needs a quota that is zero or negative and at least as large as the amount, and consumirEntrada when AGL enters, which needs a quota that is zero or positive and at least as large as the amount. Nothing resets the quota when an unlock ends. Step 1: party A buys X AGL in the official pool, which sets the quota to minus X, and closes its unlock with mint in place of take. mint moves no token (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329), so the quota stays at minus X. Step 2: party B, later in the same transaction, sells Y AGL. Its swap moves the quota to Y minus X. Its payment of Y AGL into the PoolManager calls consumirEntrada, which reverts with ForaDaCota because the quota is below Y, so the transfer and the unlock of B revert. The mirror case holds for a seller A who pays with burned claims and a buyer B whose take then fails in consumirSaida. Step 3: a party B that holds AGL claims can burn them and take up to X AGL against the quota that A left, without a swap of its own, which is the only case where a claim on AGL turns into tokens outside a swap. I read the hook source and did not simulate the two party sequence on chain. The token side of the calls is taken from the reading recorded under F-074 in cycle 4.

What is at risk: A later user of a shared transaction who trades AGL has its operation reverted and pays its gas. No balance moves to the party that left the quota, which paid the 2 percent pool fee on its own swap. Nothing outside the AGL pool is affected.

What it takes: Two parties trade AGL inside one transaction, for example two user operations of one bundle, and the first one settles its AGL side with ERC-6909 claims. The first party must hold or accept AGL claims.

What can be done: Routers that trade AGL can read cota() before the swap and refuse to proceed when it is not zero. Bundlers can keep AGL operations of different senders in separate transactions. Core cannot change the behaviour of a token hook.

This entry adds to F-074.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: unprivileged. The party that leaves the quota open is any account that trades in the AGL pool, and the author of the hook and token takes no step. The victims use a pool whose hook and token the attacker did not write, which is the definition of unprivileged in SCHEMA.md, and F-071 records the same shape (state left by an earlier party of a shared transaction) with that actor. The preconditions overstate the cost: the first party does not need to hold or accept claims, because clear (src/PoolManager.sol:310-319) forfeits a dust output and leaves the quota open in the same way, so one unit of AGL plus gas is enough. Effect degrade and severity low stand. The later trade reverts, can be repeated in another transaction, and no balance changes hands. The finding is not specific to OP Mainnet or Base and follows from no rule of those chains. The attacker must also get its call placed before the victim's call in one transaction, for example as the sender of an ERC-4337 bundle that contains the victim's operation. I did not confirm that any such carrier has handled an AGL trade.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`.

Sources:

- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x0000000456b76bc801253ef2366664750f602840 (Verified source of PoolAGL: the transient quota, afterSwap adding the negated AGL delta, consumirEntrada and consumirSaida with their sign checks, and no reset of the quota.)
- https://mainnet.optimism.io (eth_getCode of the token and the hook (15,125 and 8,730 bytes) and cota() returning 0 on OP Mainnet; the same reads on https://mainnet.base.org/ for Base.)

### F-110. Any account can call claimFor on the MortgageFi vault share mf-e-usdc-cbbtc for the PoolManager on Base, which adds 2.58 shares to the pooled balance that the caller can book through sync and settle

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Base.

What differs on the network: mf-e-usdc-cbbtc 0xe443cadb4a4fa403caee0e0546c33baff32c51f8 (name mortgagefi-earn-usdc-cbbtc, 6 decimals) is a vault share on Base that pays accrued yield as new shares. Its function claimFor(address), selector 0xddeae033, pays the yield of any holder, and the caller does not have to be that holder. The cause is the token. Base adds no network property.

How it meets the code: 1. sync stores balanceOf(PoolManager) for a currency and needs no permission (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288). _settle credits the caller with the rise of that balance since the sync (src/PoolManager.sol:349-365). 2. An eth_call from the unrelated addresses 0x00000000000000000000000000000000000a11ce and 0x00000000000000000000000000000000000b0b01, through a forwarding stub placed at the caller by state override, ran claimFor(PoolManager) at Base blocks 61,347,858 and 61,347,864 on two endpoints. balanceOf(PoolManager) went from 98.714225 to 101.294415 shares, a rise of 2.580190. claimFor for an address without shares leaves the PoolManager balance unchanged. 3. A caller that runs sync(token), claimFor(PoolManager) and settle inside one unlock is credited 2.580190 shares and can take them. I did not run that sequence against the deployed PoolManager, the statement follows from the two source ranges. 4. The yield keeps accruing on the pooled shares, so the step can be repeated later. 5. The same call pattern was run for 5,477 pooled currencies on 17 networks that have a function whose name says it acts for a holder passed as argument, and for every selector of 7,835 priced currencies. This token and the known ibBUSD (F-090) are the only ones where the PoolManager balance of a currency with a market changes. The token uniusd.fun 0xd98a61961ca8a8a53eaf48f2d6d5af5fe134c801 on Base also raises it, through collectTo(address), but that function mints 875 units to any address for any caller, so the token has no backing to lose.

What is at risk: 2.58 shares, about 2.64 USD at the GeckoTerminal price of 1.0214 USD per share, out of 98.71 shares (about 100.83 USD) that the PoolManager holds for the hookless pool 0x620bc43c2198b61932b13aabbfdba4f6e0b236d6dca7282711277856175b0895 (USDC and mf-e-usdc-cbbtc, fee 0.01 percent, tick spacing 1, no hook; the pool id equals the hash of these five values). The yield belongs to the liquidity providers of that pool in economic terms. They cannot claim it, and any account can.

What it takes: Accrued yield on the shares held by the PoolManager. It is 2.58 shares today.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should not hold yield paying vault shares in a pool when the yield can be claimed for a holder by anyone. Interfaces can flag receipt tokens that have claimFor(address).

This entry adds to F-081.

Second reviewer's corrections: The facts and the actor stand. Three additions. 1. This is the hazard of F-081 (TITHE claimFor on Robinhood Chain) and F-041 on a second token, and it should be filed as an extension of F-081 at the same severity. 2. The liquidity providers lose no principal. The 98.71 shares stay in place, and the yield could not reach them through the PoolManager in any case, since the PoolManager cannot call claim. The loss is the accrued yield, 2.58 shares today, about 2.64 USD. 3. The token admin can redirect the yield of any holder with setOverwrite(who, receiver) (source line 179, ADMIN_ROLE). If the admin sets an overwrite for the PoolManager address, claimFor mints nothing to it and the step stops working. That part has actor issuer.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`.

Sources:

- src/PoolManager.sol:279 (sync has no lock check and no permission check and stores the current balance.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:361 (paid = reservesNow - reservesBefore, credited to the recipient of settle.)
- https://mainnet.base.org/ (eth_call at block 61,347,858: claimFor(PoolManager) from two unrelated senders raises balanceOf(PoolManager) from 98714225 to 101294415; totalSupply 10721408987; stablecoin() is USDC.)
- https://base-rpc.publicnode.com (The same result at block 61,347,864.)
- https://api.geckoterminal.com/api/v2/networks/base/tokens/0xe443cadb4a4fa403caee0e0546c33baff32c51f8/pools (One pool, uniswap-v4-base, mf-e-usdc-cbbtc / USDC 0.01%, id 0x620bc43c..., token price 1.0214 USD.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/007/theme-transfer-side-effects.work/raw/hfam_8453.json:1 (The two Base hits of the full list trial: claimFor on mf-e-usdc-cbbtc and collectTo on uniusd.fun.)

### F-111. A run of dust positions bounds how far one swap can move the price inside the transaction gas cap, and building it costs a fraction of one BNB or a few POL

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Avalanche.

What differs on the network: BNB Smart Chain caps one transaction at 16,777,216 gas and Polygon at 33,554,432 gas, and Polygon charges 5460 gas for a cold storage read after PIP-88. The Avalanche C-Chain has no transaction cap below its block limit of 80,000,000 gas and charges at least half of the gas limit. Gas is cheap on all three: eth_gasPrice returned 0.05 gwei on BNB Smart Chain, 278 gwei of POL on Polygon and 7.5 gwei of AVAX on Avalanche on 2026-11-23. Ethereum has the same cap as BNB Smart Chain, and the cost of the attack in native units is what differs.

How it meets the code: 1. Pool.swap runs one loop step for each initialized tick it reaches (src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437). A step that ends on an initialized tick calls crossTick, which reads the liquidity word of the tick and reads and writes both fee growth words (src/libraries/Pool.sol:417-421 and 602-611). 2. Under Ethereum gas prices, which the BNB Smart Chain client keeps (cold read 2100, write of a non-zero slot 5000 including the cold read, fresh slot 20,000), that is three cold reads and two writes of non-zero slots, about 12,100 gas, plus the price and step arithmetic, about 16,500 gas in total. The first crossing of a tick whose fee growth words are still zero writes two fresh slots and costs about 51,000 gas. Under the Polygon prices the two figures are about 25,000 and 61,000. These figures are computed from the opcode prices in the client source and from the statements of the loop. They were not measured on chain, and the gas table of the C-Chain was not read in this cycle and is assumed equal to the Ethereum one for these opcodes. 3. One transaction can therefore cross about 1,000 already crossed ticks on BNB Smart Chain (330 on a first crossing), about 1,340 on Polygon (550) and about 4,800 on Avalanche (1,560). 4. Any account can initialize ticks. In a pool with tick spacing 1 it adds positions of one liquidity unit over consecutive ticks through modifyLiquidity (src/PoolManager.sol:145-184, src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-236). Each new tick costs the account about 70,000 gas (one position slot, one tick slot, one bitmap update) and a token amount of a few base units. 1,000 ticks cost about 70 million gas, which is 0.0035 BNB, about 19.5 POL or about 0.53 AVAX at the gas prices above. With tick spacing 1, 1,000 ticks span a price change of 10.5 percent. 5. A swap that has to move the price across more initialized ticks than the cap allows runs out of gas in every transaction. The caller can still swap in pieces, because the loop stops at sqrtPriceLimitX96 (src/libraries/Pool.sol:344 and 364) and each piece is a valid swap. A contract that needs the whole move in one call, such as a liquidation or a multi hop route with a fixed output, fails until it splits the swap. 6. The same bound applies without any attacker to a pool whose price sits far from its liquidity. An empty stretch costs one cold bitmap read and one step per word of 256 tick spacings (src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:85-119). From MIN_TICK to tick 0 a pool with spacing 1 has 3,466 words, about 17 million gas at 5,000 gas per word, which is above the BNB Smart Chain cap. Finding F-102 records that any account can move the price of an empty pool for free. 7. Liquidity removal is not affected. Pool.modifyLiquidity updates two ticks, at most two bitmap words and one position and contains no loop (src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-236), so its gas does not depend on how many ticks exist.

What is at risk: No funds are taken and no withdrawal is blocked. Swaps through the affected price range of one pool need several transactions, and integrations that swap in one call across the range revert. The cost to the attacker is a one time payment of gas. The cost to swappers repeats for each traversal. The largest Quoter simulation that cycle 5 ran on BNB Smart Chain used 11,518,424 gas, so real pools already come within a factor of 1.5 of the cap without any attack.

What it takes: A pool with a small tick spacing. Spacing 1 is the worst case because 1,000 ticks cover only 10.5 percent of price. The attacker needs gas and a few base units of both currencies. The price must have to cross the range, so the attack matters in volatile pairs and has no effect while the price stays on one side.

What can be done: Routers and liquidators should bound each swap with sqrtPriceLimitX96 and continue in a second transaction when the limit is reached. Pool creators who expect large price moves should choose a wider tick spacing, which raises the price range that a given number of ticks covers. Interfaces can read the tick bitmap through extsload to count initialized ticks on a route before sending a swap.

This entry adds to F-064.

Second reviewer's corrections: The hazard is the same on Ethereum, which has the same 2^24 cap; only the native cost of building the tick run differs, so the network difference is one of price, not of rule. The Avalanche claim that the C-Chain charges at least half of the gas limit is not supported by the lines cited and was not checked. Whether the saevm code path is the one live on C-Chain mainnet was not confirmed; the client version on the endpoint is avalanchego 1.15.3.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:602-611`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-236`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:85-119`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/v1.8.2/params/protocol_params.go (MaxTxGas is 1 << 24 (16,777,216) in the BNB Smart Chain client at tag v1.8.2, line 31, and the storage prices are the Ethereum ones: ColdSloadCostEIP2929 2100, SstoreResetGasEIP2200 5000 and SstoreSetGasEIP2200 20000, lines 67 to 72.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/0xPolygon/bor/v2.11.1/params/protocol_params.go (MaxTxGas is 1 << 25 (33,554,432) in Bor at tag v2.11.1, line 32, and the PIP-88 constants are ColdSloadCostPIP88 5460 and ColdSstoreCostPIP88 2940, lines 76 and 77.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/0xPolygon/bor/v2.11.1/core/vm/operations_acl.go (gasSLoadPIP88 charges 5460 for a cold slot and gasSStorePIP88 charges 5000 minus 2940 for a write to a warm non-zero slot.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ava-labs/avalanchego/v1.15.3/vms/saevm/worstcase/state.go (On the C-Chain a transaction is refused only when its gas limit exceeds what is left of the block limit, which is the gas rate times ten seconds (lines 123 to 155 and 295 to 297). No Osaka rule applies there.)
- rpc https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, https://polygon-bor-rpc.publicnode.com and https://api.avax.network/ext/bc/C/rpc, eth_gasPrice, eth_getBlockByNumber and eth_estimateGas on 2026-11-23; research/network-risk/cycles/008/established-l1.work/raw/fork_gas_probe.json (Block gas limits of 80,000,000, 160,000,000 and 80,000,000, gas prices of 0.05, 278 and 7.5 gwei, and the estimateGas allowance of 16777216 on BNB Smart Chain.)

### F-112. The sNVB rebase on the PoolManager balance was paid out to one contract in 469 transactions on BNB Smart Chain, which the earlier log source had not shown

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: No chain rule is involved. sNVB (0x6d6c16a3cbd318f3000e33cfd27662cf00f7e7c1) is the staked token of an Olympus fork. Every balance grows when the staking contract 0x6bcd1273eba684ff62614e41eff87123113112d1 runs a rebase, and finding F-066 records that any account can start the rebase once an epoch has ended. The PoolManager has held sNVB since block 49,283,730. Cycle 6 could not scan the history of this token, and the public log endpoint used then returns empty lists for old ranges.

How it meets the code: 1. The caller opens an unlock and calls sync(sNVB), which stores balanceOf(PoolManager) (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288). 2. It triggers the rebase. The index rises and balanceOf(PoolManager) rises with it, with no transfer. 3. It calls settle, which credits the rise since sync to the caller (src/PoolManager.sol:358-360). 4. It calls take for the same amount, which sends the tokens out (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297), and the unlock closes with all deltas at zero (src/PoolManager.sol:112). 5. The chain shows this sequence 469 times. Each of these transactions holds a LogRebase of sNVB and a transfer of sNVB from the PoolManager, and none holds a transfer to it. 468 pay the contract 0x3c69c0620ca6bd0df44c0aa8c39602f7c3ebdf27 and one pays 0xfed56cd174406e0b2ffce9c7b1ee5113e883cdab. Twelve receipts were sampled. All twelve were sent by 0x2d68a551e7c8ecf7312ee083a16e736dce2347d5 to that contract and contain no event of the PoolManager, which fits a sequence of sync, settle and take, since none of the three emits one. 6. Archive state confirms the amounts. At block 106,450,859 the index rose from 46,185,701,171 to 46,190,184,993 and the PoolManager balance stayed at 1,407,067,071,029 base units. Without an outflow it would have been 1,407,203,672,577. The difference of 136,601,548 matches the logged transfer of 136,601,545. The same holds at blocks 108,735,661 (345,465,382 against 345,465,315) and 111,270,063 (277,199,579 against 277,199,575).

What is at risk: 94.0989 sNVB left the PoolManager, between 0.127 and 0.396 sNVB per transaction. DexScreener lists no pair for the token, so no value in USD can be given, and it is likely small. The positions in sNVB pools record fixed token amounts and do not grow with a rebase (finding F-070), so the amounts paid out were backing no position. Pool reserves stay covered. The payouts stopped at block 111,270,063. The index has grown by about 0.5 percent since then, and epoch 9,503 has been due since block 135,135,655 without anyone starting it.

What it takes: The PoolManager holds the rebasing token, an epoch has ended, and the rebase can be started by any account. No role is needed. The caller pays gas only.

What can be done: Core cannot tell a rebase from a payment (assumption A2). Liquidity providers should pool the wrapped form of a rebasing token, whose balance does not move. Interfaces can warn when a currency reports a balance change without a transfer. The same advice stands in F-066 and F-091.

This entry adds to F-091.

Second reviewer's corrections: The amounts are not unpriced: the traced payout sold 136,601,545 base units of sNVB for 0.12501 BSC-USD in the same transaction through pair 0x2df8dd88da7fae1fbc995cdcd43517544b245fb3, so the total is of the order of 100 USD. Actor unprivileged stands because the token is not attacker-made and the rebase is public, but the amounts back no position (F-070), so no user balance fell; this is a token hazard outside the opcode focus of this cycle and should stay a one-line record.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- rpc https://bsc-mainnet.nodereal.io eth_getLogs in ranges of 50,000 blocks from block 49,283,000 to 135,335,758, 2026-11-23; research/network-risk/cycles/008/established-l1.work/raw/snvb_logs_nodereal_p0.json to p5.json and research/network-risk/cycles/008/established-l1.work/raw/snvb_result.json (1,814 transfers of sNVB out of the PoolManager, 973 in and 8,942 rebase logs with epochs 561 to 9,502 and no gap. 469 transactions hold a rebase log and an outflow, with recipients and amounts.)
- rpc https://bsc.api.pocket.network archive eth_call of balanceOf(PoolManager) and index() at 41 blocks and around three payout blocks, 2026-11-23; research/network-risk/cycles/008/established-l1.work/raw/snvb_grid.json and research/network-risk/cycles/008/established-l1.work/raw/snvb_payout_sample.json (The PoolManager balance and the index one block before and at three payout blocks, the 40 interval comparison of state with logs, epoch() of the staking contract with number 9,503 and end block 135,135,655, and the current balance of 38,313.74 sNVB.)
- https://api.dexscreener.com/latest/dex/tokens/0x6d6c16a3cbd318f3000e33cfd27662cf00f7e7c1 (No trading pair is listed for sNVB on 2026-11-23, so the amounts are unpriced.)

### F-115. A run of dust positions bounds how far one swap can move the price inside the gas cap, and the Quoter now measures the cost per crossing on all three chains

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Avalanche.

What differs on the network: BNB Smart Chain caps a transaction at 16,777,216 gas and Polygon at 33,554,432 gas. The C-Chain has no transaction cap below its block limit of 80,000,000 gas. Measured through the deployed Quoter on 2026-11-24, one initialized tick crossing in Pool.swap costs about 17,000 gas on BNB Smart Chain, about 27,700 on Polygon and 27,000 to 33,000 on the C-Chain, and one step over an empty bitmap word about 5,600, 9,200 and 4,900 to 6,400 gas.

How it meets the code: 1. Pool.swap runs one loop step per initialized tick and per bitmap word it reaches (src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437). A step that ends on an initialized tick calls crossTick, which reads the liquidity word and rewrites both fee growth words of the tick (src/libraries/Pool.sol:602-611). A step over an empty word reads one bitmap slot (src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:85-121). 2. This cycle replayed the loop step by step against live storage read through extsload and called quoteExactInputSingle on the deployed Quoter at the same block, for about 60 exact input amounts per pool and direction. Every quote whose state the endpoint served returned the replay's output to the unit, so the counts of crossings and empty steps per quote are exact. 3. Pool 0x628ea54a6450645d2bb7b2911c8c6f3d7f3944c2f4703c259a1b85bf5569870b (fee 2, tick spacing 1, no hook) at block 135446931: 58 Quoter calls whose output equalled the step by step replay of Pool.swap to the unit, up to 22 initialized crossings and 246 other steps in one quote. The least squares fit gives 17,020 gas per initialized crossing and 5,577 gas per step that ends on an uninitialized word boundary, with a fixed part of 41,981 gas. At 16,777,216 gas (the BNB Smart Chain transaction cap) that is about 983 crossings or about 3,000 empty words in one transaction. 4. Pool 0xa37d3e6da98dfeb7dc8103a6614f586916a6e04d41ea0a929bc19a029de1a399 (fee 8, tick spacing 1, no hook) at block 97873763: 50 Quoter calls whose output equalled the step by step replay of Pool.swap to the unit, up to 14 initialized crossings and 493 other steps in one quote. The least squares fit gives 27,683 gas per initialized crossing and 9,174 gas per step that ends on an uninitialized word boundary, with a fixed part of 54,400 gas. At 33,554,432 gas (the Polygon transaction cap) that is about 1,210 crossings or about 3,651 empty words in one transaction. 5. Pool 0x6874d3d2b99e6b75ed2fae390907e1d921d4b06b7f48fc6d3fe9c547507e369c (fee 500, tick spacing 10, no hook) at block 101248950: 118 Quoter calls whose output equalled the step by step replay of Pool.swap to the unit, up to 155 initialized crossings and 249 other steps in one quote. The least squares fit gives 27,063 gas per initialized crossing and 6,366 gas per step that ends on an uninitialized word boundary, with a constant term of -31,275 gas, so the linear fit is only approximate for small quotes. At 80,000,000 gas (the C-Chain block gas limit, which is the only bound) that is about 2,956 crossings or about 12,566 empty words in one transaction. 6. Cycle 8 computed 16,500 gas per crossing for BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche and 25,000 for Polygon. The measurement confirms BNB Smart Chain and Polygon and shows that the C-Chain costs more than its Ethereum price assumption, so the bound there is about 2,400 to 2,950 crossings per block, not 4,800. 7. As before, any account can add one unit of liquidity on consecutive ticks of a pool with tick spacing 1 to lengthen the walk, a swapper can still cross the range in several transactions with sqrtPriceLimitX96, and liquidity removal touches two ticks without a loop (src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-236).

What is at risk: No funds are taken and no withdrawal is blocked. Swaps that must cross more initialized ticks than the cap allows need several transactions, and single call integrations across that range revert. The largest single quote measured this cycle used 1,788,771 gas on BNB Smart Chain, 4,964,889 on Polygon and 5,737,630 on Avalanche.

What it takes: A pool with small tick spacing and a price move that must cross the range. The attacker pays gas and a few base units of both currencies per tick.

What can be done: Routers and liquidators should bound each swap with sqrtPriceLimitX96 and continue in another transaction. Interfaces can count initialized ticks on a route through extsload. On Avalanche the per crossing figure of this cycle should replace the computed one in any gas budget.

This entry adds to F-111.

Second reviewer's corrections: The difference is not specific to BNB Smart Chain for the cap: Ethereum enforces the same 16,777,216 cap under EIP-7825, and BNB Smart Chain charges about the Ethereum price per crossing, so on that chain the bound equals Ethereum's. Polygon has a higher cap and a higher cost per crossing; Avalanche has no lower cap. The hazard is generic to the swap loop rather than a network difference.

### F-135. From Tempo T15 a take to a zone deposit address is forwarded to a zone portal, and a failed deposit returns to the PoolManager, where an account with callbacks in the same portal batch can book it as its own payment

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: From T15, which v1.19.1 already contains and which Tempo targets for 15 December 2026, the TIP-20 precompile treats any recipient whose bytes 4 to 12 are 0xFC as a zone virtual deposit address. A transfer to it credits the zone's portal instead of the named address and then calls depositVirtual on the portal with the sender as originator. If the zone cannot deliver the deposit, the portal refunds the originator on Tempo later, minus a fee, or parks the refund for the originator to claim.

How it meets the code: An ordinary caller inside unlock calls take(token, to, amount) with a zone deposit address as to. The PoolManager debits the caller amount of the token (PoolManager.sol:291-297) and calls token.transfer(to, amount) (Currency.sol:53-80). The token debits the PoolManager amount, credits the portal amount, and calls the portal, whose code is a fixed system runtime that does not call the PoolManager. The call returns true, so the take succeeds, and the caller must settle amount as with any other take. The PoolManager loses exactly what the caller owes. If the deposit fails in the zone (unregistered master id, unconfigured key, rejected by the sequencer or the issuer policy), the portal later transfers the net amount to the PoolManager in a separate transaction, or parks it under the PoolManager's address, which the PoolManager can never claim because it makes no arbitrary calls. A refund that arrives with no sync before it raises balanceOf(PoolManager) and credits no caller (PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-364), so it stays as surplus. A reading of the portal source after the cycle (own check, 2026-11-25) adds one path. The portal pays a refund only inside processWithdrawals, which the zone's sequencer sends (ZonePortal.sol 1386-1408, 1506-1529), and claimRefund pays only its own caller (1531-1539). The same processWithdrawals call also delivers callback withdrawals: any zone account can request one (zone outbox request_withdrawal, outbox/mod.rs 159-262), and when the zone's gateway mode is open the messenger calls onWithdrawalReceived on any target (ZoneMessenger.sol 37-82). sync needs no unlock (PoolManager.sol:279) and the synced currency and reserves live in transient storage until the end of the transaction. So an account that has one callback withdrawal before the refund and one after it in the same batch can call sync(token) in the first callback and unlock, settle and take in the second. settle then counts the refund as that account's payment (PoolManager.sol:358-364).

What is at risk: No funds of liquidity providers or claim holders move. The party that named the deposit address, or the router or user it acted for, loses the refund of a failed deposit. The refund stays in the portal's refund registry, stays in the PoolManager as surplus, or goes to an account that placed callback withdrawals around it in the same portal batch. The loss to the taker is the same in all three cases.

What it takes: T15 active on mainnet with TIP-1094 in its scope (the T15 page lists only TIP-1166 so far; the code gates TIP-1094 on T15). A zone with a nonzero id below next_zone_id and a key version other than 0. For the capture path: the zone's gateway mode is open, the taker's deposit fails in the zone, and the other account's two callback withdrawals sit on either side of the refund in one processWithdrawals transaction. The zone orders its withdrawal queue, so the account cannot force that position; it can only keep callback withdrawals flowing.

What can be done: Routers and front ends should refuse take recipients whose bytes 4 to 12 are 0xFC on Tempo, or pay zone deposits from their own address so refunds return to them. Nothing in the PoolManager can be changed to stop the capture, because sync is open by design.

Second reviewer's corrections: severity: low rather than info. The difference does create an integration hazard, because a user or router that names a zone deposit address as take recipient loses the refund of a failed deposit. It is not info under SCHEMA.md since a loss path exists, but the loss falls only on the party that chose the recipient, so it does not reach liquidity providers or claim holders. The actor value unprivileged is correct for triggering it; the victims are the actor or its own users, not uninvolved users.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:53-80`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/tempo/v1.19.1/crates/precompiles/src/tip20/mod.rs (resolve_transfer routes a reserved address to portal_address(zone_id) under T15, deposit_virtual calls depositVirtual with originator equal to the sender, rejects zero amount and transfers made inside other precompiles)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/tempo/v1.19.1/crates/contracts/src/zones/address_registry.rs (zone deposit address format, magic 0xFC at bytes 4 to 12)
- https://tips.sh/1094 (refunds of failed virtual deposits go to the originator, minus the processing fee, or are parked for the originator to claim)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/upgrades.md (T15 targeted for mainnet on 15 December 2026, T14 on 8 December 2026)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/upgrades/t15.md (T15 page lists only TIP-1166 so far and says the scope may change)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/tempo/v1.19.1/crates/zones/contracts/src/runtime/tempo/ZonePortal.sol (depositVirtual 1213-1282, processWithdrawals 1386-1408 (sequencer only, reentrancy guard), deliverWithdrawal 1463-1504, _processDepositBounceBack 1506-1529, claimRefund 1531-1539 pays msg.sender only)

### F-137. A bridge payment addressed to the PoolManager, delivered by a relay that any account may execute, can be run between that account's own sync and settle and booked as its payment

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Optimism, Base, Unichain, Zora, Worldchain, Ink, Soneium, Celo, X Layer, Arbitrum One, Polygon, Avalanche, Monad, MegaETH, Arc, HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: Circle's CCTP v2 contracts are deployed at TokenMessengerV2 0x28b5a0e9C621a5BadaA536219b3a228C8168cf5d and MessageTransmitterV2 0x81D40F21F12A8F0E3252Bccb954D722d4c464B64 on 13 of the 21 networks. receiveMessage mints USDC to the mintRecipient named on the source chain inside whatever transaction calls it, and when the message's destinationCaller is zero any account may call it. On Arc the mint is native USDC, which the PoolManager also sees through the ERC-20 face at 0x3600000000000000000000000000000000000000. The same shape exists for canonical bridges. Several canonical bridges let any account run the last step of someone else's transfer in its own transaction. OP Stack L2CrossDomainMessenger.relayMessage replays a failed message for any caller (live messenger code on OP Mainnet, Base, Unichain, Zora, World Chain, Ink, Soneium, Celo and MegaETH; on X Layer its nonce is still 0). On Ethereum, OptimismPortal2.finalizeWithdrawalTransactionExternalProof finalizes any proven withdrawal for any caller, Arbitrum's Outbox.executeTransaction executes any proven L2 to L1 message, and the LxLy bridge claimAsset pays any proven deposit. X Layer runs the same LxLy bridge (networkID 3). Polygon's StateReceiver at 0x1001 replays failed state syncs for any caller. Avalanche's TeleporterMessenger accepts delivery from any relayer when the message's allowedRelayerAddresses list is empty. Each of these ends in a plain ERC-20 transfer or mint to the recipient named by the original sender.

How it meets the code: The base case is CCTP. Someone burns USDC on another chain with mintRecipient equal to this network's PoolManager and destinationCaller zero, and Circle attests the message. Any account calls unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). In the callback it calls sync(USDC) (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288), which records balanceOf(PoolManager). It then calls MessageTransmitterV2.receiveMessage(message, attestation); the destination caller check passes because the field is zero (MessageTransmitterV2.sol lines 300-305), the nonce is marked used (line 221), and TokenMessengerV2._handleReceiveMessage mints amount minus fee to the PoolManager (TokenMessengerV2.sol lines 405-422, BaseTokenMessenger.sol lines 330-355). The callback then calls settle; _settle credits the difference (src/PoolManager.sol:358-364) to the caller, which takes it out with take (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). On Arc the same sequence uses sync(0x3600...) and the face credit. CCTP v1 receiveMessage has the same destinationCaller rule (MessageTransmitter.sol lines 269-274). CCTP v2 hookData is only carried in the message; TokenMessengerV2 never executes it, so the mint recipient's own code does not run. The canonical bridge paths work the same way. The executor calls unlock, calls sync(token) in the callback (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288), then calls the relay function with the public proof or message: relayMessage of a failed message whose target is the L2 standard bridge (CrossDomainMessenger.sol lines 239-248, StandardBridge.sol lines 272-299 mints or transfers to _to with no call to it), finalizeWithdrawalTransactionExternalProof (OptimismPortal2.sol lines 701-730, which takes the prover as an argument), Outbox.executeTransaction (AbsOutbox.sol lines 158-174) reaching L1ArbitrumGateway.finalizeInboundTransfer and safeTransfer to _to (L1ArbitrumGateway.sol lines 104-125, 139-146), claimAsset (PolygonZkEVMBridgeV2.sol line 453 onward, no caller restriction), replayFailedStateSync or replayFailedStateSyncWithData (StateReceiver.sol lines 85-103) reaching the PoS ChildChainManager deposit, or receiveCrossChainMessage (TeleporterMessenger.sol lines 242 and 284, _checkIsAllowedRelayer lines 596-613) reaching an ICTT token home or remote. When the recipient is the PoolManager its token balance rises inside the callback, and settle credits the rise to the executor (src/PoolManager.sol:358-364), who takes it. Relay paths by network: CCTP receiveMessage with destinationCaller zero on Ethereum, OP Mainnet, Base, Unichain, World Chain, Ink, X Layer, Arbitrum One, Polygon, Avalanche, Monad, HyperEVM and Arc; replay of a failed L2CrossDomainMessenger message on OP Mainnet, Base, Unichain, Zora, World Chain, Ink, Soneium, Celo and MegaETH; OptimismPortal2 external-proof finalisation, Arbitrum Outbox execution and LxLy claimAsset on Ethereum; LxLy claimAsset on X Layer; StateReceiver replay on Polygon; Teleporter delivery with an empty relayer list on Avalanche. F-135 is the Tempo member of this class (zone portal refunds from the T15 fork).

What is at risk: The executor takes the bridged amount. The money belongs to whoever bridged it and named the PoolManager as recipient. No liquidity provider or claim holder funds move. A transfer of this kind that completes in a plain transaction (Circle's relayer, the first OP deposit relay, an auto-relayed Teleporter message, a claim sent by a bridge service) leaves the tokens in the PoolManager as surplus that no caller can claim, so in that case the bracket only takes money its owner had already lost, as in F-135. The loss is new only when the owner's integration planned to run the relay inside its own unlock (sync, relay, settleFor the user) and an outsider runs it first; the owner's later relay then reverts on the used nonce or spent message.

What it takes: A CCTP burn on another chain with mintRecipient equal to the PoolManager of the destination network and destinationCaller zero, and its attestation, which Circle publishes through its public attestation API. The attacker needs no role and only has to send its transaction before the intended relayer. No such mint was found on Ethereum, Unichain, Ink or World Chain (see leadResults for L-772); other networks were not enumerated. For the canonical bridges: A bridge transfer whose final recipient is the PoolManager of the destination network and whose last step is still pending: a failed OP messenger message, an unfinalized OP or Arbitrum withdrawal or unclaimed LxLy deposit to the Ethereum PoolManager, a pending failed Polygon state sync carrying a PoS deposit to the Polygon PoolManager, or a Teleporter message with no relayer restriction. No such pending item was found: the one decoded pending Polygon PoS deposit (state id 3213072) pays 0x08bb17cf80ed16483efa0217480c94c233dd9900. Other bridges were not enumerated.

What can be done: Integrations that bridge into a PoolManager should set the CCTP destinationCaller to their own receiver contract, restrict the Teleporter relayer list, or receive the funds in their own contract and pay the PoolManager inside their own sync and settle. Front ends and routers should never accept the PoolManager as a bridge recipient. Nothing in the PoolManager can change this, because sync is open by design.

Second reviewer's corrections: The handler reached on v2 is handleReceiveFinalizedMessage or handleReceiveUnfinalizedMessage depending on finalityThresholdExecuted (MessageTransmitterV2.sol lines 223-241), which both call _handleReceiveMessage. The victim is a bridger who chose the PoolManager as mintRecipient, which is an unusual act; no LP or ERC-6909 holder funds are exposed, so low is right. BNB Smart Chain and Robinhood Chain carry the code but are not listed as Circle domains; that agrees with the finding.

### F-161. Canonical routers sync before every settle on all 21 networks; one more World Chain contract settles native value without its own sync

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Optimism, Base, Unichain, Zora, Worldchain, Ink, Soneium, Celo, X Layer, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Elysium, Polygon, BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche, Monad, MegaETH, Arc, HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: The code path is the same on Ethereum. The networks differ in how often one transaction carries operations of several parties. In this cycle's sample of the newest Swap and ModifyLiquidity transactions, 195 of 250 on World Chain went to EntryPoint v0.7, 13 of about 250 on Base went to EntryPoint v0.6 or v0.8, and 1 of 126 on Tempo went to EntryPoint v0.6. EntryPoint v0.6 to v0.9 hold code on all 21 networks. Tempo stays out of the chain list because its transactions carry no native value.

How it meets the code: Unchanged from the confirmed record: a foreign sync(token) leaves the synced currency set (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, src/libraries/CurrencyReserves.sol:27-32), and a later settle with msg.value above zero by a caller that did not sync reverts with NonzeroNativeValue (src/PoolManager.sol:349-356). New in this cycle: the canonical periphery on all 21 networks syncs its own currency, native included, before every settle (DeltaResolver._settle, verified source on 44 of 61 official deployments and the same opcode sequence on 14 more), so it is immune; the top unlocker on World Chain, WorldChainSettler 0xdc7f9131df71efda6a7ae247edbe13a13c7dfd94, also syncs before both its token and its native settle (verified source lines 6566 and 6708). One more exposed contract was found by replay on World Chain: 0xabbc01621357ecb29fe89fd50a7a0b95e6e51b31 (8,899 bytes, unverified) reverted with ExecuteError(1, NonzeroNativeValue) after a foreign sync of WETH in transaction 0x8f2204591165a6ab234201905f93cedd013be497571b46f1fe24ee34602b959a, whose own value was zero, and ran again when sync(address(0)) followed the foreign sync. It pays native value from its own balance without a sync. Its caller in that transaction was its creator 0x0bee14506a83fa86c3412daef70c332fbaba7f72, so it looks like a single operator contract.

What is at risk: No funds move and none are locked. The victim's operation reverts and its gas is spent. Users of the Universal Router, the PositionManager and the 0x Settler are unaffected.

What it takes: The victim pays native value through a contract that does not sync before settle, and a foreign sync runs earlier in the same transaction with no sync or token settle in between. Not confirmed: whether 0xabbc0162 serves anyone but its creator. In the replayed transaction the creator calls 0xabbc0162 directly from an externally owned account, so no foreign frame can enter it; it is exposed only if someone routes it through a batch or a user operation.

What can be done: As recorded: integrators call sync(address(0)) before a settle with value. Accounts that batch calls into such routers can put sync(address(0)) first.

This entry adds to F-071.

Second reviewer's corrections: Contract 0xabbc0162 is not shown to have any victim. In the replayed transaction its creator calls it directly from an externally owned account, and a foreign frame cannot enter such a transaction. It can only be hit if someone routes it through a batch or a user operation. The unprivileged reach depends on the same link as the original F-071 record: the attacker has to put a frame before the victim's frame in one transaction, which in practice means building the ERC-4337 bundle. That link is not established for any network.
